# Section I: Number Theory & Arithmetic Foundations

## Chapter 1: Number System & Simplification

### Q001 · Divisibility Rules

If the 8-digit number 789x531y is divisible by 72, then what is the value of (5x - 3y)?

A. -1
B. 1
C. 7
D. 11

**Answer:** A. -1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — -1 (Correct):** A number divisible by 72 must be divisible by both 8 and 9. For divisibility by 8, the last three digits 31y must be divisible by 8; since 312 ÷ 8 = 39, we get y = 2. For divisibility by 9, the sum of digits 7 + 8 + 9 + x + 5 + 3 + 1 + 2 = 35 + x must be a multiple of 9. The only valid digit is x = 1 (making the sum 36). Therefore, 5x - 3y = 5(1) - 3(2) = 5 - 6 = -1.
- **B — 1:** Result of reversing the subtraction order to compute 3y - 5x instead of 5x - 3y.
- **C — 7:** Value obtained by adding the terms (5x + 3y) instead of subtracting them.
- **D — 11:** Result of swapping the values of x and y when substituting into 5x - 3y.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For 72 = 8 × 9, always fix the last digit first using the rule of 8 (last 3 digits), then find the middle digit using the digital sum rule of 9 by casting out 9s (7+2=9, 8+1=9, 9=9, leaving 5+3+x = 8+x → x=1).

### Q002 · Divisibility Rules

If the 9-digit number 985x3678y is divisible by 72, then what is the value of (4x - 3y)?

A. 2
B. 4
C. 8
D. 28

**Answer:** B. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 4 (Correct):** Since 72 = 8 × 9, the number must be divisible by both 8 and 9. For divisibility by 8, the last three digits 78y must be divisible by 8; 784 ÷ 8 = 98, so y = 4. For divisibility by 9, the sum of digits 9 + 8 + 5 + x + 3 + 6 + 7 + 8 + 4 = 50 + x must be a multiple of 9. The only single-digit solution is x = 4 (sum = 54). Thus, 4x - 3y = 4(4) - 3(4) = 16 - 12 = 4.
- **A — 2:** Value obtained by mistakenly taking y = 2 and adjusting x = 3 accordingly.
- **C — 8:** Result of simply adding x + y instead of evaluating 4x - 3y.
- **D — 28:** Value obtained by adding the terms (4x + 3y) instead of subtracting.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When checking divisibility by 8 on a 3-digit number abc, if a is odd, add 4 to bc and check if it is divisible by 8: here 7 is odd, so 8y + 4 must be divisible by 8 → 84 + 4 = 88, hence y = 4.

### Q003 · Divisibility Rules

If the 9-digit number 389x6378y is divisible by 72, then what is the value of √(6x + 7y)?

A. 4
B. 6
C. 8
D. 10

**Answer:** C. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 8 (Correct):** For divisibility by 72 (8 × 9), the last three digits 78y must be divisible by 8, giving y = 4 (784 ÷ 8 = 98). Next, the sum of all digits is 3 + 8 + 9 + x + 6 + 3 + 7 + 8 + 4 = 48 + x. For this to be divisible by 9, 48 + x = 54, which gives x = 6. Substituting into the expression gives √(6(6) + 7(4)) = √(36 + 28) = √64 = 8.
- **A — 4:** Result of reporting only the digit y instead of computing the radical expression.
- **B — 6:** Result of reporting only the digit x instead of computing √(6x + 7y).
- **D — 10:** Value obtained by simply adding x + y instead of evaluating √(6x + 7y).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Cast out multiples of 9 directly from the digits: (3+6)=9, 9, (8+8+7+4+3=30 → 3), leaving 3 + x = 9 → x = 6 in under 10 seconds.

### Q004 · Divisibility Rules

If the 10-digit number 1230x558y2 is divisible by 88, then for the maximum possible value of y, what is the value of (4x + 3y)?

A. 21
B. 29
C. 31
D. 25

**Answer:** D. 25
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 25 (Correct):** For divisibility by 88 (8 × 11), the last three digits 8y2 must be divisible by 8. Since 800 is divisible by 8, y2 must be divisible by 8, giving y = 3 (32) or y = 7 (72). For the maximum value of y, we take y = 7. Now check divisibility by 11 for 1230x55872: sum of digits at odd places from left = 1 + 3 + x + 5 + 7 = 16 + x; sum at even places = 2 + 0 + 5 + 8 + 2 = 17. Their difference (16 + x) - 17 = x - 1 must be 0 or a multiple of 11, so x = 1. Therefore, 4x + 3y = 4(1) + 3(7) = 25.
- **A — 21:** Result of taking the minimum possible value y = 3 (which gives x = 5) instead of the maximum value of y.
- **B — 29:** Value obtained by swapping x and y when evaluating 4x + 3y.
- **C — 31:** Result of mistakenly taking x = 0 instead of x = 1 in the divisibility-by-11 step.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For 8y2 to be divisible by 8, ignore the hundreds digit 8 and just check y2 mod 8: only 32 and 72 work, so max y = 7 immediately.

### Q005 · Divisibility Rules

If the 9-digit number 89563x87y is divisible by 72, then what is the value of √(7x - 3y)?

A. 6
B. 4
C. 8
D. 12

**Answer:** A. 6
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 6 (Correct):** For divisibility by 72 (8 × 9), the last three digits 87y must be divisible by 8; 872 ÷ 8 = 109, so y = 2. The sum of digits is 8 + 9 + 5 + 6 + 3 + x + 8 + 7 + 2 = 48 + x. For divisibility by 9, 48 + x = 54, so x = 6. Substituting these values gives √(7(6) - 3(2)) = √(42 - 6) = √36 = 6.
- **B — 4:** Result of simply subtracting x - y instead of evaluating √(7x - 3y).
- **C — 8:** Result of simply adding x + y instead of evaluating √(7x - 3y).
- **D — 12:** Value obtained by computing 2x instead of √(7x - 3y).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Since 800 is a multiple of 8, 87y is divisible by 8 if and only if 7y is divisible by 8, immediately yielding y = 2.

### Q006 · Divisibility Rules

If the 8-digit number 2074x4y2 is divisible by 88, then for the maximum possible value of y, what is the value of (4x + 3y)?

A. 29
B. 41
C. 43
D. 45

**Answer:** B. 41
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 41 (Correct):** Divisibility by 88 requires divisibility by 8 and 11. For 4y2 to be divisible by 8, y can be 3 (432 ÷ 8 = 54) or 7 (472 ÷ 8 = 59). Taking the maximum value gives y = 7. For divisibility by 11 on 2074x472, the alternating sums are (2 + 7 + x + 7) = 16 + x and (0 + 4 + 4 + 2) = 10. Their difference (16 + x) - 10 = 6 + x must be a multiple of 11, giving x = 5. Hence, 4x + 3y = 4(5) + 3(7) = 20 + 21 = 41.
- **A — 29:** Result of using the minimum possible value y = 3 (which yields x = 9) instead of the maximum y.
- **C — 43:** Value obtained by swapping x and y in the final expression 4x + 3y.
- **D — 45:** Result of using the minimum y = 3 paired with the x = 5 from the y = 7 case.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In 4y2, since 400 is divisible by 8, just check y2 mod 8 (32, 72) to get max y = 7, then pair adjacent digits for 11: (2-0)+(7-4)+(x-4)+(7-2) = x + 6 ≡ 0 (mod 11) → x = 5.

### Q007 · Divisibility Rules

If the 7-digit number 56x34y4 is divisible by 72, then what is the least possible value of (x + y)?

A. 6
B. 8
C. 5
D. 14

**Answer:** C. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 5 (Correct):** For divisibility by 72 (8 × 9), the last three digits 4y4 must be divisible by 8, which gives y = 2 (424 ÷ 8 = 53) or y = 6 (464 ÷ 8 = 58). The sum of digits is 5 + 6 + x + 3 + 4 + y + 4 = 22 + x + y. For divisibility by 9, 22 + x + y must be 27 or 36, meaning x + y = 5 or x + y = 14. When y = 2, x = 3 gives a valid digit and x + y = 5. (When y = 6, x + y = 5 is impossible for non-negative x, so x + y = 14.) Thus, the least value of x + y is 5.
- **A — 6:** Result of reporting only the digit y = 6 from the second case.
- **B — 8:** Result of reporting only the digit x = 8 from the larger solution.
- **D — 14:** Value obtained by choosing y = 6 and finding the corresponding sum x + y = 14 instead of the least value.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** You don't even need to find x separately if a valid y ≤ 5 exists: 22 + (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 9) gives x + y = 5 directly, and y = 2 ≤ 5 confirms x = 3 is valid.

### Q008 · Divisibility Rules

If the 6-digit number 47x58y is divisible by 99, then what is the value of (x × y)?

A. 12
B. 18
C. 24
D. 36

**Answer:** D. 36
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 36 (Correct):** A number divisible by 99 must be divisible by both 9 and 11. For 9, the digit sum 4 + 7 + x + 5 + 8 + y = 24 + x + y must be a multiple of 9, so x + y = 3 or x + y = 12. For 11, the difference of alternating sums is (4 + x + 8) - (7 + 5 + y) = x - y, which must be 0 (since |x - y| ≤ 9), so x = y. Thus 2x = 12 gives x = y = 6 (since 2x = 3 has no integer solution). Therefore, x × y = 6 × 6 = 36.
- **A — 12:** Result of reporting x + y instead of x × y.
- **B — 18:** Value obtained by mistakenly taking x = 3 and y = 6.
- **C — 24:** Result of taking x = 4 and y = 6 without verifying the rule of 11.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** To check divisibility by 99 quickly, break the number into 2-digit blocks from the right (47 + x5 + 8y): their sum must be a multiple of 99.

### Q009 · Divisibility Rules

If the 6-digit number 538xyz is divisible by 7, 11, and 13, then what is the value of (x² - y + z)?

A. 30
B. 14
C. 16
D. 20

**Answer:** A. 30
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 30 (Correct):** The LCM of 7, 11, and 13 is 7 × 11 × 13 = 1001. Any 6-digit number divisible by 1001 is of the form abcabc = abc × 1001, meaning the first three digits repeat in the last three places. Thus x = 5, y = 3, and z = 8. Evaluating the expression gives x² - y + z = 5² - 3 + 8 = 25 - 3 + 8 = 30.
- **B — 14:** Result of subtracting (y + z) from x² instead of computing x² - y + z.
- **C — 16:** Value obtained by simply adding x + y + z.
- **D — 20:** Result of swapping y and z to compute x² - z + y.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Memorize 7 × 11 × 13 = 1001: any 6-digit number divisible by 7, 11, and 13 simultaneously always repeats its first 3 digits in its last 3 digits.

### Q010 · Divisibility Rules

If the 6-digit number 479xyz is divisible by 7, 11, and 13, then what is the value of (y + z) / x?

A. 2
B. 4
C. 3
D. 5

**Answer:** B. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 4 (Correct):** Since LCM(7, 11, 13) = 1001, any 6-digit number 479xyz divisible by 7, 11, and 13 must repeat its first three digits, so x = 4, y = 7, and z = 9. Therefore, (y + z) / x = (7 + 9) / 4 = 16 / 4 = 4.
- **A — 2:** Result of subtracting in the numerator as (z - y) / x = 2/4, or dividing by 8 instead of 4.
- **C — 3:** Value obtained by computing (x + z) / x ≈ 3.25 or rounding (4 + 9)/4.
- **D — 5:** Result of including x in the numerator to compute (x + y + z) / x.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Whenever a 6-digit number abcxyz is divisible by 7, 11, and 13, set x = a, y = b, z = c immediately without any division.

### Q011 · Divisibility Rules

If the 9-digit number 7p5964q28 is divisible by 88, then for the maximum possible value of q, what is the value of (p² - q)?

A. 42
B. 56
C. 72
D. 90

**Answer:** C. 72
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 72 (Correct):** Divisibility by 88 requires divisibility by 8 and 11. For q28 to be divisible by 8, 100q + 28 ≡ 4q + 4 ≡ 0 (mod 8), so q must be odd (1, 3, 5, 7, 9). For the maximum value, q = 9 (928 ÷ 8 = 116). Now for divisibility by 11 on 7p5964928, the alternating sums are (7 + 5 + 6 + 9 + 8) = 35 and (p + 9 + 4 + 2) = 15 + p. Their difference 35 - (15 + p) = 20 - p must be 11, so p = 9. Thus, p² - q = 9² - 9 = 81 - 9 = 72.
- **A — 42:** Result of taking the next largest valid odd digit q = 7 (which gives p = 7) instead of maximum q = 9.
- **B — 56:** Result of mistakenly taking q = 8 and p = 8.
- **D — 90:** Value obtained by adding p² + q instead of subtracting q from p².
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For q28 to be divisible by 8, since 28 ≡ 4 (mod 8), q must be odd so that 4q ≡ 4 (mod 8); thus max q = 9 instantly.

### Q012 · Divisibility Rules

What is the sum of all possible values of k for which the 8-digit number 647k8236 is divisible by 12?

A. 9
B. 12
C. 15
D. 18

**Answer:** D. 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 18 (Correct):** Divisibility by 12 requires divisibility by both 4 and 3. The last two digits 36 are already divisible by 4, so any digit k satisfies divisibility by 4. For divisibility by 3, the sum of digits 6 + 4 + 7 + k + 8 + 2 + 3 + 6 = 36 + k must be a multiple of 3. Since 36 is already a multiple of 3, k can be 0, 3, 6, or 9. Their sum is 0 + 3 + 6 + 9 = 18.
- **A — 9:** Result of reporting only the largest possible value of k instead of the sum of all values.
- **B — 12:** Value obtained by omitting k = 6 or counting only 3 + 9.
- **C — 15:** Result of miscalculating the fixed digit sum as 37 instead of 36 (giving k ∈ {2, 5, 8}).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When the remaining digits already sum to a multiple of 3, the possible values of a single digit are always {0, 3, 6, 9}, whose sum is always 18.

### Q013 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of (7⁹⁵ - 3⁵⁸).

A. 4
B. 6
C. 0
D. 8

**Answer:** A. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 4 (Correct):** Both 7 and 3 have a unit-digit cyclicity of 4. For 7^95: 95 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 3, so the unit digit of 7^95 matches 7^3 = 343, which is 3. For 3^58: 58 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 2, so the unit digit of 3^58 matches 3^2 = 9. Subtracting the unit digits gives 3 - 9, so we borrow 10 to get 13 - 9 = 4.
- **B — 6:** Classic trap of taking the absolute difference |3 - 9| = 6 instead of borrowing 10.
- **C — 0:** Result of miscomputing the exponent remainders modulo 4 as 1 and 1.
- **D — 8:** Result of taking 7^2 - 3^0 or misapplying the cyclicity of 3.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When subtracting unit digits and the first digit is smaller (3 - 9), always borrow 10 (13 - 9 = 4) rather than taking |3 - 9| = 6.

### Q014 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of (2467)¹⁵³ × (342)⁷².

A. 4
B. 2
C. 7
D. 8

**Answer:** B. 2
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2 (Correct):** We only need the unit digits of the bases: 7^153 × 2^72. Both 7 and 2 have a cyclicity of 4. For 7^153, 153 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 1, so the unit digit is 7^1 = 7. For 2^72, 72 is exactly divisible by 4 (remainder 0), so we use the 4th power: 2^4 = 16, giving unit digit 6. Multiplying the unit digits gives 7 × 6 = 42, so the unit digit is 2.
- **A — 4:** Result of using 2^1 = 2 instead of 2^4 = 6 when the exponent is divisible by 4.
- **C — 7:** Result of mistakenly setting 2^72 ≡ 2^0 = 1, giving 7 × 1 = 7.
- **D — 8:** Value obtained by misreading 153 mod 4 as 2, giving 9 × 2 = 18.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When the exponent is a multiple of 4, never replace it with 0; replace it with 4 (so 2^72 ends in 2^4 = 6, not 2^0 = 1).

### Q015 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

What is the unit digit of 1! + 2! + 3! + 4! + ... + 100!?

A. 9
B. 1
C. 3
D. 5

**Answer:** C. 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 3 (Correct):** For every integer n ≥ 5, n! contains at least one factor of 5 and 2, so its unit digit is 0. Thus, we only need the sum of the first four factorials: 1! + 2! + 3! + 4! = 1 + 2 + 6 + 24 = 33. The unit digit of the entire sum is 3.
- **A — 9:** Result of mistakenly taking 4! = 4 instead of 24 (1 + 2 + 6 + 4 = 13? Wait: 1 + 2 + 6 = 9 if 4! is omitted).
- **B — 1:** Result of forgetting 2! = 2 and 3! = 6 or miscomputing 1 + 2 + 6 + 24.
- **D — 5:** Result of taking 5! as ending in 5 instead of 0.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Memorize 1! + 2! + 3! + 4! = 33; since every factorial from 5! onward ends in 0, any sum 1! + 2! + ... + n! (for n ≥ 4) always ends in 3.

### Q016 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of 1⁵ + 2⁵ + 3⁵ + ... + 20⁵.

A. 5
B. 2
C. 4
D. 0

**Answer:** D. 0
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 0 (Correct):** Since the cyclicity of every digit divides 4, n^5 always has the same unit digit as n^1 = n. Thus the unit digit of 1^5 + 2^5 + ... + 20^5 equals the unit digit of 1 + 2 + 3 + ... + 20. Using n(n + 1)/2, the sum is (20 × 21)/2 = 210, whose unit digit is 0.
- **A — 5:** Result of summing only the first 10 terms (1 + 2 + ... + 10 = 55).
- **B — 2:** Result of miscomputing 20 × 21 without dividing by 2 or taking n^4 instead.
- **C — 4:** Value obtained by dropping the 10s digits incorrectly during summation.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For any integer n, n^5 ≡ n (mod 10), so the sum of 5th powers from 1 to 10k always has unit digit 0.

### Q017 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of (4387)²⁴⁵ × (621)⁷² + (198)¹⁰¹.

A. 5
B. 6
C. 3
D. 9

**Answer:** A. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 5 (Correct):** Find the unit digit of each part: for 4387^245, 245 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 1, so 7^1 = 7. For 621^72, any power of 1 ends in 1. For 198^101, 101 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 1, so 8^1 = 8. Combining them: 7 × 1 + 8 = 15, which has a unit digit of 5.
- **B — 6:** Result of taking 8^101 to end in 6 (even-power cyclicity) instead of 8.
- **C — 3:** Value obtained by using 7^3 = 3 instead of 7^1 = 7.
- **D — 9:** Result of taking 1^72 as 2 or misadding 7 + 2.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Check the last two digits of the exponent mod 4: 45 ≡ 1 (mod 4) and 01 ≡ 1 (mod 4), so both 7 and 8 stay at power 1.

### Q018 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of (164)¹⁶⁹ + (333)³³⁷ - (727)⁷²⁶.

A. 2
B. 8
C. 6
D. 4

**Answer:** B. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 (Correct):** For base ending in 4, an odd power gives unit digit 4, so 164^169 ends in 4. For 333^337, 37 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 1, so 3^1 = 3. For 727^726, 26 ÷ 4 leaves remainder 2, so 7^2 = 49, giving unit digit 9. Combining: 4 + 3 - 9 = 7 - 9; borrowing 10 gives 17 - 9 = 8.
- **A — 2:** Trap of taking |7 - 9| = 2 instead of borrowing 10 to compute 17 - 9.
- **C — 6:** Result of using 4^(even) = 6 instead of 4^(odd) = 4, giving 6 + 3 - 9 = 0 or similar error.
- **D — 4:** Value obtained by taking 7^26 mod 4 incorrectly as 7^1 = 7.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** 4^(odd) = 4 and 4^(even) = 6; combine that with mod-4 checks on the last two digits of the other exponents (37 → 1, 26 → 2).

### Q019 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of (1!)¹⁰⁰ + (2!)⁹⁹ + (3!)⁹⁸ + (4!)⁹⁷ + ... + (100!)¹.

A. 1
B. 3
C. 9
D. 5

**Answer:** C. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 9 (Correct):** For n ≥ 5, n! ends in 0, so (n!)^k also ends in 0. Thus we only evaluate the first four terms: (1!)^100 = 1^100 = 1; (2!)^99 = 2^99, where 99 ≡ 3 (mod 4) so 2^3 = 8; (3!)^98 = 6^98, which always ends in 6; (4!)^97 = 24^97, where 4^(odd) ends in 4. Summing the unit digits gives 1 + 8 + 6 + 4 = 19, so the unit digit is 9.
- **A — 1:** Result of treating 4^97 as an even power (6) instead of an odd power (4), giving 1 + 8 + 6 + 6 = 21.
- **B — 3:** Result of ignoring the exponents and simply adding 1! + 2! + 3! + 4! = 33.
- **D — 5:** Value obtained by taking 2^99 ≡ 2^2 = 4 instead of 2^3 = 8.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Any term from 5! onward contributes 0, and any positive power of 6 ends in 6, leaving only 2^99 and 4^97 to check.

### Q020 · Unit Digit Cyclicity

Find the unit digit of 17¹⁹⁹⁹ + 11¹⁹⁹⁹ - 7¹⁹⁹⁹.

A. 3
B. 7
C. 5
D. 1

**Answer:** D. 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 1 (Correct):** Both 17^1999 and 7^1999 have bases ending in 7 and identical exponents (1999), so they share the exact same unit digit and cancel each other out in subtraction. The remaining term is 11^1999, and any positive power of 1 ends in 1. Thus, the unit digit is 1.
- **A — 3:** Result of computing only 7^1999 (7^3 = 343 → 3) and forgetting the cancellation.
- **B — 7:** Result of adding the unit digits (3 + 1 + 3 = 7) instead of subtracting 7^1999.
- **C — 5:** Value obtained by miscomputing 7^1999 - 7^1999 + 1^1999 with a borrowing error.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Spot identical base unit digits with equal exponents: 17^1999 and 7^1999 cancel immediately, leaving only 11^1999 → 1.

### Q021 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the total number of factors (divisors) of 1440.

A. 36
B. 30
C. 24
D. 18

**Answer:** A. 36
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 36 (Correct):** Prime factorize 1440: 1440 = 144 × 10 = (2^4 × 3^2) × (2 × 5) = 2^5 × 3^2 × 5^1. The total number of factors is given by the product of (exponent + 1) for each prime factor: (5 + 1)(2 + 1)(1 + 1) = 6 × 3 × 2 = 36.
- **B — 30:** Result of multiplying the exponents directly (5 × 2 × 1) or forgetting +1 on one prime factor.
- **C — 24:** Result of mis-factorizing 1440 as 2^3 × 3^2 × 5^1.
- **D — 18:** Value obtained by forgetting the +1 on 5^1, computing 6 × 3 × 1.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Split 1440 into 144 × 10 = (16 × 9) × (2 × 5) = 2^5 × 3^2 × 5^1, then multiply (5+1)(2+1)(1+1) = 36.

### Q022 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

How many odd factors does 2160 have?

A. 40
B. 8
C. 32
D. 6

**Answer:** B. 8
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 (Correct):** Prime factorize 2160: 2160 = 216 × 10 = (2^3 × 3^3) × (2 × 5) = 2^4 × 3^3 × 5^1. To count only odd factors, exclude all powers of 2 and use only the odd prime factors: (3 + 1)(1 + 1) = 4 × 2 = 8.
- **A — 40:** Result of computing the total number of factors (4+1)(3+1)(1+1) = 40 instead of only odd factors.
- **C — 32:** Result of computing the number of even factors 4 × (3+1)(1+1) = 32 instead of odd factors.
- **D — 6:** Value obtained by forgetting to add +1 to the exponents of 3 and 5, computing 3 × 1 = 3 or 3 × 2 = 6.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For odd factors, completely ignore the power of 2 in the prime factorization and multiply (exponent + 1) of only the odd primes.

### Q023 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the number of even factors of 1800.

A. 36
B. 9
C. 27
D. 18

**Answer:** C. 27
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 27 (Correct):** Prime factorize 1800: 1800 = 18 × 100 = (2 × 3^2) × (2^2 × 5^2) = 2^3 × 3^2 × 5^2. An even factor must contain at least one 2, so the exponent of 2 can be 1, 2, or 3 (3 choices, not 4). Thus, the number of even factors is 3 × (2 + 1) × (2 + 1) = 3 × 3 × 3 = 27.
- **A — 36:** Result of computing the total number of factors 4 × 3 × 3 = 36 instead of only even factors.
- **B — 9:** Result of computing the number of odd factors 3 × 3 = 9 instead of even factors.
- **D — 18:** Value obtained by multiplying 3 × 3 × 2 without adding +1 to the exponent of 5.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For even factors, do NOT add 1 to the exponent of 2; multiply (exponent of 2) × (other exponents + 1).

### Q024 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

How many factors of 3600 are multiples of 15?

A. 45
B. 16
C. 30
D. 20

**Answer:** D. 20
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 20 (Correct):** Prime factorize 3600: 3600 = 36 × 100 = (2^2 × 3^2) × (2^2 × 5^2) = 2^4 × 3^2 × 5^2. To count factors that are multiples of 15 = 3^1 × 5^1, factor out 15: 3600 = 15 × (2^4 × 3^1 × 5^1). The number of such factors equals the total factors of (2^4 × 3^1 × 5^1), which is (4 + 1)(1 + 1)(1 + 1) = 5 × 2 × 2 = 20.
- **A — 45:** Result of computing all factors of 3600 (5 × 3 × 3 = 45) without restricting to multiples of 15.
- **B — 16:** Result of forgetting +1 on the exponent of 2, computing 4 × 2 × 2 = 16.
- **C — 30:** Value obtained by subtracting only 1 from one exponent instead of both 3 and 5.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Factor out the required divisor (15 = 3 × 5) from the prime factorization first, then count the total factors of whatever remains.

### Q025 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the sum of all factors of 360.

A. 1170
B. 1092
C. 810
D. 1260

**Answer:** A. 1170
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1170 (Correct):** Prime factorize 360: 360 = 36 × 10 = 2^3 × 3^2 × 5^1. The sum of all factors is (2^0 + 2^1 + 2^2 + 2^3)(3^0 + 3^1 + 3^2)(5^0 + 5^1) = (1 + 2 + 4 + 8)(1 + 3 + 9)(1 + 5) = 15 × 13 × 6 = 1170.
- **B — 1092:** Result of omitting 2^0 = 1 in the first bracket, giving 14 × 13 × 6 = 1092.
- **C — 810:** Result of omitting the 3^0 + 3^1 terms or using 3^2 = 9 instead of 1 + 3 + 9 = 13.
- **D — 1260:** Value obtained by miscomputing 15 × 13 as 210 instead of 195.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use the GP sum formula (p^(a+1) - 1)/(p - 1) for each prime power: (16-1)/1 × (27-1)/2 × (25-1)/4 = 15 × 13 × 6 = 1170.

### Q026 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the total number of prime factors of 6¹⁰ × 7¹⁷ × 11²⁷.

A. 54
B. 64
C. 4
D. 58

**Answer:** B. 64
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 64 (Correct):** Rewrite the expression in terms of prime bases: 6^10 × 7^17 × 11^27 = (2 × 3)^10 × 7^17 × 11^27 = 2^10 × 3^10 × 7^17 × 11^27. The total number of prime factors (counted with multiplicity) is the sum of the exponents of all prime bases: 10 + 10 + 17 + 27 = 64.
- **A — 54:** Trap of adding the given exponents (10 + 17 + 27) without splitting the composite base 6 = 2 × 3.
- **C — 4:** Result of counting only the number of distinct prime factors (2, 3, 7, 11) instead of total prime factors.
- **D — 58:** Value obtained from an arithmetic slip when adding 20 + 17 + 27.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Never sum exponents before breaking composite bases into primes: 6^10 contributes 2 × 10 = 20 prime factors, not 10.

### Q027 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

How many factors of N = 2⁶ × 3⁴ × 5³ are perfect squares?

A. 12
B. 6
C. 24
D. 140

**Answer:** C. 24
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 24 (Correct):** A factor 2^a × 3^b × 5^c of N = 2^6 × 3^4 × 5^3 is a perfect square if and only if a, b, and c are all even. Valid choices are a ∈ {0, 2, 4, 6} (4 choices), b ∈ {0, 2, 4} (3 choices), and c ∈ {0, 2} (2 choices). Thus, the number of perfect square factors is 4 × 3 × 2 = 24.
- **A — 12:** Result of forgetting to add 1 for the zero exponent on the last prime or miscounting c.
- **B — 6:** Result of forgetting to add +1 (for exponent 0) to any of the halved exponents, giving 3 × 2 × 1 = 6.
- **D — 140:** Result of computing the total number of factors (7 × 5 × 4) instead of only perfect square factors.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For perfect square factors, divide each prime exponent by 2 (taking the floor), add 1, and multiply: (⌊6/2⌋+1)(⌊4/2⌋+1)(⌊3/2⌋+1) = 4 × 3 × 2 = 24.

### Q028 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the number of trailing zeros in 250!.

A. 50
B. 60
C. 63
D. 62

**Answer:** D. 62
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 62 (Correct):** The number of trailing zeros in n! equals the highest power of 5 dividing n!. Using Legendre's formula: ⌊250/5⌋ + ⌊250/25⌋ + ⌊250/125⌋ = 50 + 10 + 2 = 62.
- **A — 50:** Result of dividing by 5 only once (250 ÷ 5 = 50) and ignoring higher powers of 5.
- **B — 60:** Result of forgetting the ⌊250/125⌋ = 2 contribution.
- **C — 63:** Value obtained by mistakenly adding 1 for 250 itself.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Keep dividing the quotient by 5 successively and add the integer quotients: 250 ÷ 5 = 50; 50 ÷ 5 = 10; 10 ÷ 5 = 2 → 50 + 10 + 2 = 62.

### Q029 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the number of trailing zeros in the product 10 × 20 × 30 × ... × 500.

A. 62
B. 50
C. 60
D. 12

**Answer:** A. 62
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 62 (Correct):** Factor out 10 from each of the 50 terms: 10 × 20 × 30 × ... × 500 = 10^50 × (1 × 2 × 3 × ... × 50) = 10^50 × 50!. The term 10^50 gives 50 trailing zeros. In 50!, the number of trailing zeros is ⌊50/5⌋ + ⌊50/25⌋ = 10 + 2 = 12. Total trailing zeros = 50 + 12 = 62.
- **B — 50:** Result of counting only the 10^50 factor and ignoring the extra zeros generated inside 50!.
- **C — 60:** Result of forgetting ⌊50/25⌋ = 2 when counting zeros in 50!.
- **D — 12:** Result of counting only the trailing zeros in 50! and forgetting the 50 zeros from 10^50.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Pull out 10^50 first (50 zeros), then add the trailing zeros of 50! (10 + 2 = 12) for a total of 62.

### Q030 · Factors & Trailing Zeros

Find the number of trailing zeros in 1¹ × 2² × 3³ × ... × 25²⁵.

A. 75
B. 100
C. 50
D. 105

**Answer:** B. 100
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 100 (Correct):** Since 2s are far more abundant than 5s, we count the total exponent of 5 in 1^1 × 2^2 × ... × 25^25. Only multiples of 5 contribute: 5^5 gives 5 fives, 10^10 gives 10 fives, 15^15 gives 15 fives, 20^20 gives 20 fives, and 25^25 = (5^2)^25 = 5^50 gives 50 fives. Total = 5 + 10 + 15 + 20 + 50 = 100.
- **A — 75:** Classic trap of counting 25^25 as contributing only 25 fives instead of 50 fives.
- **C — 50:** Result of missing the 20^20 term or counting only 5+10+15+20.
- **D — 105:** Value obtained from an arithmetic slip when summing the series.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Sum the exponents of multiples of 5 (5+10+15+20+25 = 75), then add an extra 25 for 25^25 (since 25 = 5^2) to get 100.

### Q031 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when (17²⁰⁰ + 19²⁰⁰) is divided by 18.

A. 0
B. 1
C. 2
D. 17

**Answer:** C. 2
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 2 (Correct):** Using negative and positive remainders modulo 18: 17 ≡ -1 (mod 18) and 19 ≡ 1 (mod 18). Therefore, 17^200 + 19^200 ≡ (-1)^200 + 1^200 = 1 + 1 = 2 (mod 18). The remainder is 2.
- **A — 0:** Trap of treating (-1)^200 as -1 (as if the power were odd), giving -1 + 1 = 0.
- **B — 1:** Result of forgetting one of the two terms in the sum.
- **D — 17:** Result of miscomputing (-1)^200 + 1^200 as -1 modulo 18.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Replace 17 with -1 and 19 with +1 modulo 18; since 200 is even, (-1)^200 + 1^200 = 1 + 1 = 2 immediately.

### Q032 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when 7¹⁰¹ is divided by 48.

A. 1
B. 41
C. 47
D. 7

**Answer:** D. 7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 7 (Correct):** Rewrite 7^101 as 7 × (7^2)^50 = 7 × 49^50. Since 49 ≡ 1 (mod 48), we have 7 × 49^50 ≡ 7 × 1^50 = 7 (mod 48). Thus, the remainder is 7.
- **A — 1:** Result of forgetting the leftover factor of 7 when rewriting 7^101 as 7 × 49^50.
- **B — 41:** Result of treating 49 ≡ -1 (mod 48) instead of +1, giving -7 ≡ 41.
- **C — 47:** Result of assuming 7^101 ≡ -1 (mod 48).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Pair 7s into 7^2 = 49 ≡ 1 (mod 48); the single leftover 7 is the exact remainder.

### Q033 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when 2⁵⁰ is divided by 15.

A. 4
B. 1
C. 2
D. 8

**Answer:** A. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 4 (Correct):** Notice that 2^4 = 16 ≡ 1 (mod 15). Rewrite 2^50 as (2^4)^12 × 2^2 = 16^12 × 4. Modulo 15, this becomes 1^12 × 4 = 4. Hence, the remainder is 4.
- **B — 1:** Result of forgetting the leftover 2^2 factor after reducing (2^4)^12 to 1.
- **C — 2:** Result of reporting the exponent remainder (50 mod 4 = 2) instead of 2^2 = 4.
- **D — 8:** Value obtained by miscomputing 50 mod 4 as 3, giving 2^3 = 8.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide the exponent 50 by 4 (since 2^4 = 16 ≡ 1 mod 15) to get remainder 2, so 2^50 ≡ 2^2 = 4 (mod 15).

### Q034 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when (5⁷⁰ + 7⁷⁰) is divided by 74.

A. 2
B. 0
C. 12
D. 24

**Answer:** B. 0
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 0 (Correct):** Rewrite the expression as (5^2)^35 + (7^2)^35 = 25^35 + 49^35. Because the exponent 35 is odd, a^n + b^n is always divisible by (a + b). Here a + b = 25 + 49 = 74, so 25^35 + 49^35 is completely divisible by 74, leaving remainder 0.
- **A — 2:** Result of misapplying negative/positive remainders to get 1 + 1 = 2.
- **C — 12:** Result of simply adding the bases 5 + 7 = 12.
- **D — 24:** Result of taking the difference 49 - 25 = 24.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Square both bases to match the divisor: 5^2 + 7^2 = 25 + 49 = 74, and since the remaining exponent 35 is odd, a^35 + b^35 is divisible by a + b = 74.

### Q035 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when 2¹⁰⁰ is divided by 101.

A. 2
B. 0
C. 1
D. 100

**Answer:** C. 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1 (Correct):** Since 101 is a prime number and gcd(2, 101) = 1, Fermat's Little Theorem states that a^(p-1) ≡ 1 (mod p). Here p = 101, so 2^100 ≡ 1 (mod 101). The remainder is 1.
- **A — 2:** Result of confusing a^(p-1) ≡ 1 with a^p ≡ a (mod p).
- **B — 0:** Result of assuming the power is divisible by 101.
- **D — 100:** Result of confusing +1 with -1 (100 mod 101).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** By Fermat's Little Theorem, whenever p is prime and doesn't divide a, a^(p-1) ÷ p always leaves remainder 1.

### Q036 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when 3⁴² is divided by 10.

A. 1
B. 3
C. 7
D. 9

**Answer:** D. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 9 (Correct):** Using Euler's Totient Theorem, φ(10) = 10(1 - 1/2)(1 - 1/5) = 4, and gcd(3, 10) = 1, so 3^4 ≡ 1 (mod 10). Rewrite 3^42 as (3^4)^10 × 3^2 ≡ 1^10 × 9 = 9 (mod 10). The remainder is 9.
- **A — 1:** Result of ignoring the leftover 3^2 factor after applying 3^4 ≡ 1 (mod 10).
- **B — 3:** Result of using 3^1 instead of 3^2 for the remaining exponent.
- **C — 7:** Value obtained by miscomputing 42 mod 4 as 3, giving 3^3 = 27 → 7.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Remainder when divided by 10 is simply the unit digit of 3^42: 42 mod 4 = 2 → 3^2 = 9.

### Q037 · Remainder Theorem

(49¹⁵ - 1) is completely divisible by which of the following numbers?

A. 48
B. 50
C. 14
D. 35

**Answer:** A. 48
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 48 (Correct):** For any positive integer n, a^n - b^n is always divisible by (a - b). Setting a = 49, b = 1, and n = 15, (49^15 - 1^15) is guaranteed to be divisible by (49 - 1) = 48. None of 14, 35, or 50 divides 49^15 - 1.
- **B — 50:** Result of confusing a^n - 1 with a^n + 1 (which would be divisible by 49 + 1 = 50 for odd n).
- **C — 14:** Result of testing 49 mod 14 = 7, forgetting 7^15 - 1 is not 0 mod 14.
- **D — 35:** Result of mistakenly factoring 49 as 7 × 7 and assuming divisibility by 35.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** a^n - 1 is ALWAYS divisible by (a - 1) for every integer n ≥ 1; here 49 - 1 = 48.

### Q038 · Remainder Theorem

A number when successively divided by 4, 5, and 7 leaves remainders 2, 3, and 4 respectively. What will be the remainder when the same number is divided by 140?

A. 95
B. 94
C. 92
D. 114

**Answer:** B. 94
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 94 (Correct):** To find the least such number, work backward from the last divisor 7 with quotient 0: 7(0) + 4 = 4. Next, with divisor 5 and remainder 3: 5(4) + 3 = 23. Finally, with divisor 4 and remainder 2: 4(23) + 2 = 94. Since 4 × 5 × 7 = 140, any such number is of the form 140k + 94, so dividing by 140 leaves remainder 94.
- **A — 95:** Trap of reversing the order of divisors (starting from 4 instead of 7): 7(5(2)+3)+4 = 95.
- **C — 92:** Result of missing the final remainder 2 or misadding 4 × 23.
- **D — 114:** Value obtained by adding 20 to 94 from a quotient slip.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use the ladder method bottom-up: start with 0, multiply by 7 and add 4 → 4; multiply by 5 and add 3 → 23; multiply by 4 and add 2 → 94.

### Q039 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when (1! + 2! + 3! + ... + 50!) is divided by 12.

A. 6
B. 0
C. 9
D. 3

**Answer:** C. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 9 (Correct):** Since 4! = 24 is divisible by 12, every factorial n! for n ≥ 4 is also divisible by 12 and leaves remainder 0. Thus, we only need the remainder of 1! + 2! + 3! = 1 + 2 + 6 = 9 when divided by 12, which is 9.
- **A — 6:** Result of mistakenly evaluating 3! as 3 instead of 6, giving 1 + 2 + 3 = 6.
- **B — 0:** Result of assuming the entire sum is divisible by 12 because 50! is divisible by 12.
- **D — 3:** Value obtained by subtracting 6 from 9 or miscomputing 9 mod 12.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Once n! becomes a multiple of the divisor (here 4! = 24 is a multiple of 12), ignore all terms from 4! onward.

### Q040 · Remainder Theorem

Find the remainder when (17²³ + 29²³) is divided by 23.

A. 46
B. 2
C. 17
D. 0

**Answer:** D. 0
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 0 (Correct):** Since the exponent 23 is odd, a^n + b^n is always divisible by (a + b). Here, 17^23 + 29^23 is divisible by 17 + 29 = 46. Because 23 divides 46, the expression is also divisible by 23, leaving remainder 0.
- **A — 46:** Result of applying Fermat's Little Theorem (a^22 ≡ 1) to get 17 + 29 = 46 and forgetting to reduce 46 mod 23.
- **B — 2:** Result of mistakenly setting 17^23 ≡ 1 and 29^23 ≡ 1, giving 1 + 1 = 2.
- **C — 17:** Result of reducing only 29 ≡ 6 (mod 23) and losing track of the powers.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** 17 + 29 = 46 = 2 × 23; since the exponent 23 is odd, a^23 + b^23 is divisible by 46 and therefore by 23, so remainder = 0.

### Q041 · AP, GP & Number Series

How many numbers between 200 and 600 (both inclusive) are divisible by both 4 and 6?

A. 34
B. 33
C. 17
D. 25

**Answer:** A. 34
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 34 (Correct):** Numbers divisible by both 4 and 6 are multiples of LCM(4, 6) = 12. The first multiple of 12 ≥ 200 is 12 × 17 = 204, and the last multiple ≤ 600 is 12 × 50 = 600. The total count is 50 - 17 + 1 = 34.
- **B — 33:** Classic fencepost error: computing 50 - 17 = 33 without adding 1.
- **C — 17:** Trap of using 4 × 6 = 24 instead of LCM(4, 6) = 12, giving ⌊600/24⌋ - ⌊199/24⌋ = 25 - 8 = 17.
- **D — 25:** Result of reporting ⌊600/24⌋ = 25.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Count multiples of LCM(4, 6) = 12 using ⌊600/12⌋ - ⌊199/12⌋ = 50 - 16 = 34.

### Q042 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the value of 5² + 6² + 7² + ... + 15².

A. 1185
B. 1210
C. 1240
D. 1140

**Answer:** B. 1210
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1210 (Correct):** Use the sum of squares formula Σk² = n(n + 1)(2n + 1)/6. For n = 15: (15 × 16 × 31)/6 = 5 × 8 × 31 = 1240. For n = 4: (4 × 5 × 9)/6 = 30. Subtracting gives 1240 - 30 = 1210.
- **A — 1185:** Result of subtracting S(5) = 55 instead of S(4) = 30, which drops the 5² term.
- **C — 1240:** Result of computing S(15) = 1240 and forgetting to subtract 1² + 2² + 3² + 4².
- **D — 1140:** Value obtained from a calculation error in (15 × 16 × 31)/6.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Compute S(15) - S(4) using n(n+1)(2n+1)/6: 1240 - 30 = 1210.

### Q043 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the sum of the infinite series: 12 + 4 + 4/3 + 4/9 + ... ∞.

A. 9
B. 16
C. 18
D. 24

**Answer:** C. 18
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 18 (Correct):** This is an infinite geometric progression with first term a = 12 and common ratio r = 4/12 = 1/3. Since |r| < 1, the sum is S_∞ = a / (1 - r) = 12 / (1 - 1/3) = 12 / (2/3) = 18.
- **A — 9:** Result of using 1 + r in the denominator instead of 1 - r: 12 / (4/3) = 9.
- **B — 16:** Result of multiplying 12 × (4/3) = 16.
- **D — 24:** Result of taking r = 1/2 instead of 1/3, giving 12 / (1/2) = 24.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use S_∞ = a / (1 - r): 12 / (2/3) = 18 in 5 seconds.

### Q044 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the value of 1/(1×2) + 1/(2×3) + 1/(3×4) + ... + 1/(99×100).

A. 100/101
B. 101/100
C. 1/100
D. 99/100

**Answer:** D. 99/100
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 99/100 (Correct):** Split each fraction using 1/[k(k+1)] = 1/k - 1/(k+1): (1 - 1/2) + (1/2 - 1/3) + ... + (1/99 - 1/100). All intermediate terms cancel out, leaving 1 - 1/100 = 99/100.
- **A — 100/101:** Result of misreading the last denominator as 100 × 101 instead of 99 × 100.
- **B — 101/100:** Result of adding 1 + 1/100 instead of subtracting, or inverting the difference.
- **C — 1/100:** Result of keeping only the last term 1/100.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For telescoping series starting at 1/(1×2) and ending at 1/(n×(n+1)), the sum is simply n/(n+1) = 99/100.

### Q045 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the value of 1/(3×5) + 1/(5×7) + 1/(7×9) + ... + 1/(19×21).

A. 1/7
B. 2/7
C. 1/21
D. 4/21

**Answer:** A. 1/7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 1/7 (Correct):** The difference between factors in each denominator is 2. Using the telescoping formula (1/d) × (1/first - 1/last): (1/2) × (1/3 - 1/21) = (1/2) × (6/21) = 3/21 = 1/7.
- **B — 2/7:** Classic trap of forgetting to multiply by (1 / common_diff) = 1/2, which gives 6/21 = 2/7.
- **C — 1/21:** Result of keeping only the last term 1/21.
- **D — 4/21:** Value obtained by miscomputing 1/3 - 1/21 as 8/21.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always use (1 / common_diff) × (1/first - 1/last) = (1/2)(1/3 - 1/21) = 1/7.

### Q046 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the sum of 1 - 2 + 3 - 4 + 5 - 6 + ... + 99 - 100.

A. 50
B. -50
C. -100
D. -49

**Answer:** B. -50
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — -50 (Correct):** Group the 100 terms into 50 consecutive pairs: (1 - 2) + (3 - 4) + (5 - 6) + ... + (99 - 100). Each pair evaluates to -1. Thus, the total sum is 50 × (-1) = -50.
- **A — 50:** Result of making a sign error and reporting +50 instead of -50.
- **C — -100:** Result of multiplying (-1) by 100 terms instead of 50 pairs.
- **D — -49:** Result of miscounting the number of pairs as 49.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For 1 - 2 + 3 - 4 + ... - 2n, the sum is simply -n = -50.

### Q047 · AP, GP & Number Series

Find the value of 1² - 2² + 3² - 4² + ... + 9² - 10².

A. 55
B. -45
C. -55
D. -110

**Answer:** C. -55
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — -55 (Correct):** Pair the terms and apply a² - b² = (a - b)(a + b): (1 - 2)(1 + 2) + (3 - 4)(3 + 4) + ... + (9 - 10)(9 + 10) = -(1 + 2 + 3 + ... + 10). Using n(n + 1)/2, this is -(10 × 11)/2 = -55.
- **A — 55:** Result of forgetting the negative sign from (1 - 2) = -1.
- **B — -45:** Result of summing only from 1 to 9 instead of 1 to 10.
- **D — -110:** Result of forgetting to divide by 2 in the sum-of-natural-numbers formula n(n+1)/2.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** 1² - 2² + 3² - ... - n² (for even n) equals -(1 + 2 + ... + n) = -n(n+1)/2 = -55.

### Q048 · AP, GP & Number Series

In an Arithmetic Progression, the 5th term is 19 and the 9th term is 35. Find the sum of its first 12 terms.

A. 324
B. 312
C. 282
D. 300

**Answer:** D. 300
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 300 (Correct):** In an AP, T_5 = a + 4d = 19 and T_9 = a + 8d = 35. Subtracting gives 4d = 16, so d = 4. Substituting back gives a = 19 - 16 = 3. The sum of the first 12 terms is S_12 = (12/2)[2(3) + (12 - 1)(4)] = 6[6 + 44] = 6 × 50 = 300.
- **A — 324:** Result of using n×d (12 × 4 = 48) instead of (n - 1)d (11 × 4 = 44) in the sum formula.
- **B — 312:** Result of miscomputing a = 4 instead of 3.
- **C — 282:** Result of miscomputing d or omitting the factor of 2 in 2a.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Find d = (35 - 19)/(9 - 5) = 4, then a = 3, and S_12 = 6 × (2a + 11d) = 300.

### Q049 · Recurring Decimals

Express 0.42̅3̅ in the form p/q (in lowest terms).

A. 419/990
B. 423/990
C. 419/999
D. 419/900

**Answer:** A. 419/990
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 419/990 (Correct):** Using the standard conversion rule for mixed recurring decimals: write the full digits minus the non-repeating part in the numerator, and in the denominator put one 9 for each repeating digit followed by one 0 for each non-repeating digit after the decimal. Thus, 0.42323... = (423 - 4) / 990 = 419 / 990. Since 419 is not divisible by 2, 3, 5, or 11, the fraction is in lowest terms.
- **B — 423/990:** Result of forgetting to subtract the non-repeating digit 4 from the numerator.
- **C — 419/999:** Result of using 999 in the denominator instead of 990 (ignoring the non-repeating digit 4).
- **D — 419/900:** Result of reversing the count of 9s and 0s to write 900 in the denominator.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Rule: (Full number - non-repeating digits) / (as many 9s as repeating digits, followed by as many 0s as non-repeating decimal digits) = (423 - 4)/990.

### Q050 · Recurring Decimals

Find the value of 0.6̅3̅ + 0.3̅7̅ + 0.8̅0̅.

A. 1.80
B. 1.8̅1̅
C. 1.8̅0̅
D. 1.8̅2̅

**Answer:** B. 1.8̅1̅
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 1.8̅1̅ (Correct):** Convert each recurring decimal to a fraction: 0.6̅3̅ = 63/99, 0.3̅7̅ = 37/99, and 0.8̅0̅ = 80/99. Adding them gives (63 + 37 + 80)/99 = 180/99 = 1 + 81/99 = 1.8̅1̅.
- **A — 1.80:** Trap of adding them like terminating decimals (0.63 + 0.37 + 0.80 = 1.80) and ignoring the repeating carry.
- **C — 1.8̅0̅:** Result of adding 180/99 incorrectly as 1 + 80/99.
- **D — 1.8̅2̅:** Value obtained by miscomputing 180 - 99 as 82.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Convert to /99 fractions: (63 + 37 + 80)/99 = 180/99 = 1 + 81/99 = 1.8̅1̅. Notice 180/99 has a carry of 1 into the repeating block!

### Q051 · Recurring Decimals

Find the value of 3.3̅6̅ - 2.0̅5̅ + 1.3̅3̅.

A. 2.7̅4̅
B. 1.6̅4̅
C. 2.6̅4̅
D. 2.5̅4̅

**Answer:** C. 2.6̅4̅
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 2.6̅4̅ (Correct):** Separate the integer and recurring fractional parts: (3 + 36/99) - (2 + 5/99) + (1 + 33/99) = (3 - 2 + 1) + (36 - 5 + 33)/99 = 2 + 64/99 = 2.6̅4̅.
- **A — 2.7̅4̅:** Result of adding 5 instead of subtracting 5 in the fractional numerator.
- **B — 1.6̅4̅:** Result of dropping 1 from the integer part calculation.
- **D — 2.5̅4̅:** Result of miscomputing 36 - 5 + 33 as 54.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Operate on integer parts and /99 numerators separately when no borrow or carry occurs: 3 - 2 + 1 = 2, and 36 - 5 + 33 = 64 → 2.6̅4̅.

### Q052 · Recurring Decimals

Find the value of 0.4̅ × 0.3̅.

A. 0.1̅2̅
B. 0.12
C. 0.1̅3̅
D. 0.1̅4̅8̅

**Answer:** D. 0.1̅4̅8̅
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 0.1̅4̅8̅ (Correct):** Convert both recurring decimals to fractions: 0.4̅ = 4/9 and 0.3̅ = 3/9. Multiply them: (4/9) × (3/9) = 12/81 = 4/27. To write 4/27 as a recurring decimal, multiply numerator and denominator by 37: (4 × 37)/(27 × 37) = 148/999 = 0.1̅4̅8̅.
- **A — 0.1̅2̅:** Trap of multiplying the repeating digits directly as 4 × 3 = 12 to write 0.1̅2̅.
- **B — 0.12:** Result of treating them as terminating decimals (0.4 × 0.3 = 0.12).
- **C — 0.1̅3̅:** Value obtained by dividing 4 ÷ 27 inaccurately and rounding.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always convert recurring decimals to fractions before multiplying! Remember 27 × 37 = 999 to convert /27 to recurring decimals instantly.

### Q053 · Recurring Decimals

Find the value of √(0.4̅).

A. 0.6̅
B. 0.2̅
C. 0.2
D. 0.8̅

**Answer:** A. 0.6̅
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 0.6̅ (Correct):** Convert 0.4̅ to a fraction: 0.4̅ = 4/9. Taking the square root gives √(4/9) = 2/3 = 0.6̅.
- **B — 0.2̅:** Trap of taking √4 = 2 directly to write 0.2̅ instead of converting 0.4̅ = 4/9 first.
- **C — 0.2:** Result of treating 0.4̅ as terminating 0.4 and guessing 0.2.
- **D — 0.8̅:** Result of doubling 0.4̅ instead of taking its square root.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Convert 0.4̅ = 4/9 first; √(4/9) = 2/3 = 0.6̅. Never take the square root of the digit 4 directly!

### Q054 · Recurring Decimals

Find the value of 0.47̅ + 0.50̅3̅ - 0.39̅ in fraction form (lowest terms).

A. 116/198
B. 115/198
C. 113/198
D. 58/99

**Answer:** B. 115/198
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 115/198 (Correct):** Convert each term to a fraction with common denominator 990: 0.47̅ = (47 - 4)/90 = 43/90 = 473/990; 0.50̅3̅ = (503 - 5)/990 = 498/990; 0.39̅ = (39 - 3)/90 = 36/90 = 396/990. Combining them: (473 + 498 - 396)/990 = 575/990. Dividing numerator and denominator by 5 gives 115/198.
- **A — 116/198:** Result of forgetting to subtract 5 in 0.50̅3̅ (using 503/990 instead of 498/990).
- **C — 113/198:** Value obtained from a subtraction slip in 473 + 498 - 396.
- **D — 58/99:** Result of simplifying 575/990 inaccurately.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use 990 as the common denominator for 1-digit and 2-digit repeating blocks, then simplify by 5 at the end.

### Q055 · Surds & Indices

Which of the following is the largest among ∛4, ∜6, ⁶√15, and ¹²√245?

A. ¹²√245
B. ⁶√15
C. ∛4
D. ∜6

**Answer:** C. ∛4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — ∛4 (Correct):** The LCM of the radical orders (3, 4, 6, 12) is 12. Raise each surd to the 12th power: (∛4)^12 = 4^4 = 256; (∜6)^12 = 6^3 = 216; (⁶√15)^12 = 15^2 = 225; (¹²√245)^12 = 245^1 = 245. Since 256 is the largest, ∛4 is the largest surd.
- **A — ¹²√245:** Trap of choosing the largest number inside the radical (245) without adjusting for the root order.
- **B — ⁶√15:** Result of comparing 15^2 = 225 inaccurately or picking the middle term.
- **D — ∜6:** Result of comparing bases (6 > 4) without raising to the 12th power.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Raise all surds to the LCM of their roots (12): 4^4=256, 6^3=216, 15^2=225, 245^1=245 → ∛4 wins.

### Q056 · Surds & Indices

Which of the following is the smallest among 2²⁵⁰, 3¹⁵⁰, 5¹⁰⁰, and 4²⁰⁰?

A. 2²⁵⁰
B. 3¹⁵⁰
C. 4²⁰⁰
D. 5¹⁰⁰

**Answer:** D. 5¹⁰⁰
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 5¹⁰⁰ (Correct):** Take the HCF of the exponents (250, 150, 100, 200), which is 50. Rewrite each number as a 50th power: 2^250 = (2^5)^50 = 32^50; 3^150 = (3^3)^50 = 27^50; 5^100 = (5^2)^50 = 25^50; 4^200 = (4^4)^50 = 256^50. Since 25 is the smallest base, 5^100 is the smallest number.
- **A — 2²⁵⁰:** Trap of picking the smallest base (2) without accounting for its large exponent 250.
- **B — 3¹⁵⁰:** Result of picking 3^150 by miscomputing 3^3 or 5^2.
- **C — 4²⁰⁰:** Result of confusing smallest with largest (4^200 = 256^50).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide all exponents by their HCF (50) to reduce them to 2^5=32, 3^3=27, 5^2=25, and 4^4=256.

### Q057 · Surds & Indices

Simplify: √(11 + 2√30).

A. √6 + √5
B. √10 + 1
C. √6 - √5
D. √15 + √2

**Answer:** A. √6 + √5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — √6 + √5 (Correct):** Write 11 + 2√30 in the form (a + b) + 2√(ab). We need two numbers whose sum is 11 and product is 30, which are 6 and 5. Thus, √(11 + 2√30) = √((√6 + √5)²) = √6 + √5.
- **B — √10 + 1:** Result of picking factors of 30 that add to 11 incorrectly, such as 10 + 1 (product 10 ≠ 30).
- **C — √6 - √5:** Result of using a minus sign instead of a plus sign.
- **D — √15 + √2:** Result of picking factors 15 and 2 (sum 17 ≠ 11).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For √(S + 2√P), just find two numbers adding to S and multiplying to P (6 + 5 = 11, 6 × 5 = 30) → √6 + √5.

### Q058 · Surds & Indices

Find the value of 1/(√100 + √99) + 1/(√99 + √98) + ... + 1/(√2 + √1).

A. 11
B. 9
C. 10
D. 99

**Answer:** B. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 9 (Correct):** Rationalize each denominator by multiplying by its conjugate: 1/(√100 + √99) = √100 - √99, 1/(√99 + √98) = √99 - √98, and so on down to 1/(√2 + √1) = √2 - √1. Summing all terms cancels every intermediate radical, leaving √100 - √1 = 10 - 1 = 9.
- **A — 11:** Result of adding √100 + √1 = 10 + 1 instead of subtracting.
- **C — 10:** Result of forgetting to subtract √1 = 1 from √100 = 10.
- **D — 99:** Result of subtracting 100 - 1 without taking square roots.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When all terms are + and denominators are √(n+1) + √n, the sum collapses to √(largest) - √(smallest) = 10 - 1 = 9.

### Q059 · Surds & Indices

If 2ˣ⁺³ + 2ˣ⁺¹ = 320, then what is the value of x?

A. 6
B. 4
C. 5
D. 7

**Answer:** C. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 5 (Correct):** Factor out 2^(x+1) from the left-hand side: 2^(x+1)(2^2 + 1) = 320, so 2^(x+1) × 5 = 320. Dividing by 5 gives 2^(x+1) = 64 = 2^6. Equating exponents gives x + 1 = 6, so x = 5.
- **A — 6:** Result of equating x = 6 directly from 2^6 instead of solving x + 1 = 6.
- **B — 4:** Result of miscomputing 320 ÷ 5 or solving x + 2 = 6.
- **D — 7:** Result of adding 1 to 6 instead of subtracting 1.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Factor out the smaller power 2^(x+1): 2^(x+1) × 5 = 320 → 2^(x+1) = 64 = 2^6 → x = 5.

### Q060 · Surds & Indices

Find the value of √(12 + √(12 + √(12 + ... ∞))).

A. 3
B. 6
C. 12
D. 4

**Answer:** D. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4 (Correct):** Let y = √(12 + √(12 + ...)). Squaring both sides gives y² = 12 + y, or y² - y - 12 = 0. Factoring gives (y - 4)(y + 3) = 0. Since y > 0, we get y = 4.
- **A — 3:** Trap of choosing the smaller consecutive factor 3, which is the answer when the signs are minus (-).
- **B — 6:** Result of dividing 12 by 2.
- **C — 12:** Result of confusing the infinite sum radical with the infinite product √(12√(12√12...)) = 12.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Split 12 into consecutive factors 3 × 4; for '+' signs take the larger factor (4), and for '-' signs take the smaller factor (3).

### Q061 · Surds & Indices

Which of the following is the greatest?

A. √11 - √9
B. √13 - √11
C. √15 - √13
D. √17 - √15

**Answer:** A. √11 - √9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — √11 - √9 (Correct):** Notice that the difference under the square roots is constant (2) in every option. Rationalizing each expression gives 2 / (√(n+2) + √n). Since the numerator is constant (2), the expression with the smallest denominator is the greatest. Thus, √11 - √9 is the greatest.
- **B — √13 - √11:** Trap of assuming larger numbers under the radicals produce a larger difference.
- **C — √15 - √13:** Result of choosing a middle pair without rationalizing.
- **D — √17 - √15:** Result of choosing another middle pair without rationalizing.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When differences are equal in √a - √b, smaller numbers give a LARGER difference! So √11 - √9 is greatest.

### Q062 · BODMAS & Algebraic Simplification

Simplify: 72 ÷ 9 of 2 + 18 ÷ 3 × 2 - 4 of 3 ÷ 6.

A. 26
B. 14
C. 5
D. 17

**Answer:** B. 14
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 14 (Correct):** Follow BODMAS strictly, evaluating 'of' before division and multiplication: 9 of 2 = 18 and 4 of 3 = 12. The expression becomes 72 ÷ 18 + 18 ÷ 3 × 2 - 12 ÷ 6. Next evaluate ÷ and × from left to right: 4 + (6 × 2) - 2 = 4 + 12 - 2 = 14.
- **A — 26:** Classic trap of treating 'of' as ordinary multiplication after division: (72 ÷ 9) × 2 = 16, giving 16 + 12 - 2 = 26.
- **C — 5:** Result of doing multiplication before division in 18 ÷ 3 × 2 (= 18 ÷ 6 = 3), giving 4 + 3 - 2 = 5.
- **D — 17:** Result of making both the 'of' error and the multiplication-before-division error.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always convert 'a of b' into (a × b) as a single grouped unit before performing any ÷ or ×.

### Q063 · BODMAS & Algebraic Simplification

If 1 / (1 + 1 / (1 + 1 / (1 + 1/2))) = x/y in lowest terms, then what is the value of (x + y)?

A. 8
B. 21
C. 13
D. 11

**Answer:** C. 13
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 13 (Correct):** Evaluate the continued fraction from the bottom up: 1 + 1/2 = 3/2; then 1 + 1/(3/2) = 1 + 2/3 = 5/3; then 1 + 1/(5/3) = 1 + 3/5 = 8/5; finally 1/(8/5) = 5/8. Since x/y = 5/8 in lowest terms, x = 5 and y = 8, so x + y = 13.
- **A — 8:** Result of reporting only the denominator y = 8 instead of x + y.
- **B — 21:** Result of adding an extra '1 +' step at the top to get 13/8, giving 13 + 8 = 21.
- **D — 11:** Value obtained from an addition error when computing 1 + 3/5.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use the ladder trick starting with 1, 2: next terms by adding previous two are 3, 5, 8 → 5/8, so 5 + 8 = 13.

### Q064 · BODMAS & Algebraic Simplification

Simplify: [(0.87)³ + (0.13)³] / [(0.87)² - 0.87 × 0.13 + (0.13)²].

A. 0.74
B. 0.1
C. 1.13
D. 1

**Answer:** D. 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 1 (Correct):** Let a = 0.87 and b = 0.13. The expression has the form (a³ + b³) / (a² - ab + b²), which simplifies identically to (a + b). Thus, 0.87 + 0.13 = 1.
- **A — 0.74:** Trap of computing a - b = 0.87 - 0.13 = 0.74 instead of a + b.
- **B — 0.1:** Result of misplacing the decimal point when adding 0.87 + 0.13.
- **C — 1.13:** Result of misadding 0.87 + 0.13 as 1.13.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Spot (a³ + b³)/(a² - ab + b²) = a + b immediately: 0.87 + 0.13 = 1 in 3 seconds.

### Q065 · BODMAS & Algebraic Simplification

Simplify: (1 - 1/2²)(1 - 1/3²)(1 - 1/4²) ... (1 - 1/20²).

A. 21/40
B. 19/40
C. 21/20
D. 1/20

**Answer:** A. 21/40
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 21/40 (Correct):** Factor each term using 1 - 1/k² = (k - 1)(k + 1)/k². The product becomes (1×3)/(2×2) × (2×4)/(3×3) × (3×5)/(4×4) × ... × (19×21)/(20×20). All intermediate factors cancel, leaving the first left-factor (1/2) and the last right-factor (21/20): (1/2) × (21/20) = 21/40.
- **B — 19/40:** Result of using (n - 1)/(2n) instead of (n + 1)/(2n).
- **C — 21/20:** Result of forgetting the 1/2 factor from the first bracket.
- **D — 1/20:** Result of confusing this with the product of (1 - 1/k) from k = 2 to 20, which gives 1/20.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For the product of (1 - 1/k²) from k = 2 to n, the result is always (n + 1) / (2n) = 21/40.

---

## Chapter 2: LCM & HCF

### Q066 · LCM × HCF = Product

The HCF and LCM of two numbers are 18 and 378 respectively. If one of the numbers is 54, what is the other number?

A. 126
B. 108
C. 162
D. 189

**Answer:** A. 126
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 126 (Correct):** Using the fundamental property Product of two numbers = HCF × LCM: 54 × Other number = 18 × 378. Dividing both sides by 54 gives Other number = (18 × 378) / 54 = 378 / 3 = 126. Verification: HCF(54, 126) = 18 and LCM(54, 126) = 378.
- **B — 108:** Result of doubling the given number 54 instead of using the HCF × LCM relationship.
- **C — 162:** Result of multiplying 54 by 3 instead of dividing 378 by 3.
- **D — 189:** Result of dividing the LCM (378) by 2 instead of dividing by 3 (54 ÷ 18).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide the given number by HCF first (54 ÷ 18 = 3), then divide LCM by that quotient (378 ÷ 3 = 126) to avoid multiplying large numbers.

### Q067 · LCM × HCF = Product

The LCM of two numbers is 12 times their HCF. The sum of the HCF and LCM is 403. If one of the numbers is 93, find the other number.

A. 124
B. 155
C. 186
D. 248

**Answer:** A. 124
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 124 (Correct):** Let HCF = h, so LCM = 12h. Given h + 12h = 403, which gives 13h = 403, so h = 403 / 13 = 31. Thus LCM = 12 × 31 = 372. Using Product = HCF × LCM: 93 × Other number = 31 × 372. Therefore, Other number = (31 × 372) / 93 = 372 / 3 = 124.
- **B — 155:** Result of multiplying HCF (31) by 5 instead of 4 due to miscalculating 12 ÷ 3.
- **C — 186:** Result of dividing the LCM (372) by 2 instead of 3 (93 ÷ 31).
- **D — 248:** Result of doubling the correct answer (124 × 2) instead of taking 4 × 31.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Express LCM + HCF as (k + 1) × HCF to find HCF in one division (403 ÷ 13 = 31), then Other number = (k × HCF²) ÷ First number.

### Q068 · LCM × HCF = Product

The sum of two numbers is 135, their HCF is 9, and their LCM is 396. What is the sum of their reciprocals?

A. 7/396
B. 15/44
C. 5/132
D. 1/44

**Answer:** C. 5/132
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 5/132 (Correct):** Let the two numbers be a and b. We are given a + b = 135, HCF = 9, and LCM = 396. Since a × b = HCF × LCM = 9 × 396 = 3564, the sum of their reciprocals is (1/a) + (1/b) = (a + b) / (a × b) = 135 / (9 × 396) = 15 / 396 = 5 / 132. (The numbers are 36 and 99.)
- **A — 7/396:** Result of computing the difference of reciprocals (1/36 − 1/99 = 7/396) instead of their sum.
- **B — 15/44:** Result of forgetting to multiply LCM by HCF in the denominator, computing 135 / 396 = 15/44.
- **D — 1/44:** Result of dividing HCF by LCM (9 / 396 = 1/44) instead of Sum / (HCF × LCM).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Directly use Sum of reciprocals = Sum of numbers / (HCF × LCM) without ever solving for the individual numbers.

### Q069 · LCM × HCF = Product

The LCM of two numbers is 45 times their HCF. If one of the numbers is 125 and the sum of their HCF and LCM is 1150, what is the other number?

A. 175
B. 200
C. 250
D. 225

**Answer:** D. 225
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 225 (Correct):** Let HCF = H, so LCM = 45H. Given H + 45H = 1150, so 46H = 1150, giving H = 25. Thus LCM = 45 × 25 = 1125. Using Product = HCF × LCM: 125 × Other number = 25 × 1125, so Other number = (25 × 1125) / 125 = 1125 / 5 = 225.
- **A — 175:** Result of multiplying HCF (25) by 7 instead of 9.
- **B — 200:** Result of multiplying HCF (25) by 8 instead of 9 when dividing 45 by 5.
- **C — 250:** Result of doubling the given number 125 instead of using the HCF × LCM formula.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Since 125 = 5 × 25 and LCM = 45 × 25, the other number is simply (45 ÷ 5) × 25 = 9 × 25 = 225.

### Q070 · LCM × HCF = Product

The HCF and LCM of two numbers are 16 and 640 respectively. If the difference between the two numbers is 48, what is their sum?

A. 192
B. 208
C. 224
D. 240

**Answer:** B. 208
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 208 (Correct):** Let the numbers be 16x and 16y where x and y are co-prime. Given 16xy = LCM = 640, so xy = 40. Also 16(x − y) = 48, so x − y = 3. Using (x + y)² = (x − y)² + 4xy = 3² + 4(40) = 9 + 160 = 169, we get x + y = 13. Thus the sum of the numbers is 16(x + y) = 16 × 13 = 208. (The numbers are 128 and 80.)
- **A — 192:** Result of taking x + y = 12 instead of 13 when computing √169.
- **C — 224:** Result of taking x + y = 14 instead of 13.
- **D — 240:** Result of taking x + y = 15 instead of 13.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Factor out HCF first: xy = LCM/HCF = 40 and x − y = 48/16 = 3; mentally spot 8 × 5 = 40 with 8 − 5 = 3, so Sum = 16 × (8 + 5) = 208.

### Q071 · LCM × HCF = Product

If A = 2³ × 3² × 5⁴ and B = 2² × 3⁴ × 5² × 7, then what is the ratio of the LCM of A and B to the HCF of A and B?

A. 450 : 1
B. 1575 : 1
C. 3150 : 1
D. 6300 : 1

**Answer:** C. 3150 : 1
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 3150 : 1 (Correct):** For prime-factorized numbers, LCM takes the highest power of each prime and HCF takes the lowest common power. Thus LCM(A, B) = 2³ × 3⁴ × 5⁴ × 7 and HCF(A, B) = 2² × 3² × 5². Dividing LCM by HCF gives 2^(3−2) × 3^(4−2) × 5^(4−2) × 7 = 2¹ × 3² × 5² × 7 = 2 × 9 × 25 × 7 = 3150. Hence the ratio is 3150 : 1.
- **A — 450 : 1:** Result of omitting the prime factor 7 when computing the LCM of A and B.
- **B — 1575 : 1:** Result of dropping the factor of 2¹ when subtracting exponents of 2.
- **D — 6300 : 1:** Result of multiplying by an extra factor of 2 (using 2² instead of 2¹ in the quotient).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Subtract the smaller exponent from the larger exponent for each prime directly: 2¹ × 3² × 5² × 7¹ = 450 × 7 = 3150.

### Q072 · LCM × HCF = Product

The product of two numbers is 4107. If the HCF of these numbers is 37, then the greater number is:

A. 74
B. 107
C. 148
D. 111

**Answer:** D. 111
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 111 (Correct):** Let the numbers be 37x and 37y where x and y are co-prime. Their product is 37x × 37y = 4107, so 1369xy = 4107, which gives xy = 4107 / 1369 = 3. Since x and y are co-prime positive integers, the only pair is (1, 3). Thus the numbers are 37 and 37 × 3 = 111, and the greater number is 111.
- **A — 74:** Result of multiplying the HCF (37) by 2 instead of 3.
- **B — 107:** Result of an arithmetic slip when dividing 4107 by 37.
- **C — 148:** Result of multiplying the HCF (37) by 4 instead of 3.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide the product by HCF² immediately: 4107 ÷ 37² = 111 ÷ 37 = 3, so the greater number is 37 × 3 = 111.

### Q073 · LCM × HCF = Product

The LCM of two numbers is 20 times their HCF. The difference between the LCM and HCF is 456. If one of the numbers is 96, what is the other number?

A. 120
B. 144
C. 160
D. 192

**Answer:** A. 120
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 120 (Correct):** Let HCF = H, so LCM = 20H. Given LCM − HCF = 456, we have 19H = 456, so H = 456 / 19 = 24, and LCM = 20 × 24 = 480. Using Product = HCF × LCM: 96 × Other number = 24 × 480, so Other number = (24 × 480) / 96 = 480 / 4 = 120.
- **B — 144:** Result of multiplying HCF (24) by 6 instead of 5.
- **C — 160:** Result of dividing the LCM (480) by 3 instead of 4 (96 ÷ 24).
- **D — 192:** Result of doubling the given number 96 instead of using the HCF × LCM identity.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Since 96 = 4 × 24 and LCM = 20 × 24, the other number is (20 ÷ 4) × 24 = 5 × 24 = 120.

### Q074 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

Two numbers are in the ratio 5 : 7. If their HCF is 19, what is their LCM?

A. 570
B. 665
C. 760
D. 931

**Answer:** B. 665
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 665 (Correct):** Let the two numbers be 5x and 7x where x is their HCF. Given HCF = 19, the numbers are 5 × 19 = 95 and 7 × 19 = 133. Their LCM = HCF × 5 × 7 = 19 × 35 = 665.
- **A — 570:** Result of multiplying 19 × 30 instead of 19 × 35.
- **C — 760:** Result of multiplying 19 × 40 instead of 19 × 35.
- **D — 931:** Result of multiplying 19 by 7² (49) instead of 5 × 7 (35).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For numbers in co-prime ratio a : b with HCF = H, directly compute LCM = H × a × b = 19 × 35 = 665.

### Q075 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

Three numbers are in the ratio 2 : 3 : 5 and their LCM is 3600. What is their HCF?

A. 60
B. 90
C. 120
D. 180

**Answer:** C. 120
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 120 (Correct):** Let the three numbers be 2h, 3h, and 5h where h is their HCF. Since 2, 3, and 5 are pairwise co-prime, LCM(2h, 3h, 5h) = (2 × 3 × 5)h = 30h. Given 30h = 3600, we get h = 3600 / 30 = 120.
- **A — 60:** Result of dividing 3600 by 60 instead of 30.
- **B — 90:** Result of dividing 3600 by 40 instead of 30.
- **D — 180:** Result of dividing 3600 by 20 after missing a factor of 3 or 2 in the ratio product.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide the LCM by the LCM of the ratio terms: HCF = 3600 ÷ LCM(2, 3, 5) = 3600 ÷ 30 = 120.

### Q076 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The sum of two numbers is 216 and their HCF is 27. How many such pairs of numbers are possible?

A. 1
B. 2
C. 3
D. 4

**Answer:** B. 2
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 2 (Correct):** Let the numbers be 27x and 27y where x and y are co-prime positive integers. Given 27x + 27y = 216, dividing by 27 gives x + y = 8. Checking all unordered pairs (x, y) summing to 8: (1, 7) is co-prime; (2, 6) has gcd 2; (3, 5) is co-prime; (4, 4) has gcd 4. Thus, only 2 pairs are possible: (27, 189) and (81, 135).
- **A — 1:** Result of missing one of the two valid co-prime pairs, such as (3, 5).
- **C — 3:** Result of counting the non-coprime pair (2, 6) along with the valid pairs.
- **D — 4:** Result of counting all four unordered positive integer pairs summing to 8 without checking the co-prime condition.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide Sum by HCF (216 ÷ 27 = 8), then count only co-prime pairs (x, 8 − x) with x ≤ 4: (1, 7) and (3, 5) → 2 pairs.

### Q077 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The product of two numbers is 4320 and their HCF is 12. How many such pairs of numbers are possible?

A. 1
B. 2
C. 3
D. 4

**Answer:** D. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4 (Correct):** Let the numbers be 12x and 12y where x and y are co-prime positive integers. Their product is 12x × 12y = 4320, so 144xy = 4320, which gives xy = 4320 / 144 = 30. Factorizing 30 into co-prime unordered pairs (x, y) gives: (1, 30), (2, 15), (3, 10), and (5, 6). All 4 pairs have gcd(x, y) = 1, so 4 pairs of numbers are possible.
- **A — 1:** Result of counting only the trivial pair (1, 30) and missing interior factorizations.
- **B — 2:** Result of missing two of the four co-prime factorizations of 30.
- **C — 3:** Result of overlooking one of the four co-prime factor pairs of 30.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Compute k = Product ÷ HCF² = 4320 ÷ 144 = 30. Since 30 = 2 × 3 × 5 has 3 distinct prime factors, the number of co-prime pairs is 2^(3−1) = 4.

### Q078 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The LCM of two numbers is 495 and their HCF is 5. If the sum of the two numbers is 100, what is their difference?

A. 10
B. 15
C. 20
D. 25

**Answer:** A. 10
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 (Correct):** Let the numbers be 5x and 5y with gcd(x, y) = 1. Then LCM = 5xy = 495 implies xy = 99, and 5(x + y) = 100 implies x + y = 20. Using (x − y)² = (x + y)² − 4xy = 20² − 4(99) = 400 − 396 = 4, we get x − y = 2. Hence the difference between the two numbers is 5(x − y) = 5 × 2 = 10. (The numbers are 55 and 45.)
- **B — 15:** Result of computing 3 × 5 instead of 2 × 5 for the difference.
- **C — 20:** Result of reporting (x + y) = 20 instead of scaling (x − y) by the HCF 5.
- **D — 25:** Result of an arithmetic slip when evaluating √((x + y)² − 4xy).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide both LCM and Sum by HCF (xy = 99, x + y = 20); spot 11 × 9 = 99 and 11 + 9 = 20, so difference = 5 × (11 − 9) = 10.

### Q079 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

Two numbers are in the ratio 3 : 4. The product of their HCF and LCM is 2028. What is the sum of the two numbers?

A. 78
B. 84
C. 91
D. 104

**Answer:** C. 91
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 91 (Correct):** Let the two numbers be 3h and 4h where h is their HCF. Since Product = HCF × LCM = 2028, we have 3h × 4h = 2028, so 12h² = 2028, giving h² = 169 and h = 13. The two numbers are 3 × 13 = 39 and 4 × 13 = 52, so their sum is 39 + 52 = 91.
- **A — 78:** Result of computing 6 × 13 instead of (3 + 4) × 13.
- **B — 84:** Result of taking h = 12 instead of 13 when solving 12h² = 2028.
- **D — 104:** Result of computing 8 × 13 instead of 7 × 13.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** HCF = √(Product ÷ (3 × 4)) = √(2028 ÷ 12) = √169 = 13, so Sum = (3 + 4) × 13 = 91.

### Q080 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The HCF of two numbers, each greater than 29, is 29, and their LCM is 4147. What is the sum of the two numbers?

A. 638
B. 667
C. 725
D. 696

**Answer:** D. 696
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 696 (Correct):** Let the two numbers be 29x and 29y with gcd(x, y) = 1. Since LCM = 29xy = 4147, we get xy = 4147 / 29 = 143 = 11 × 13. Because both numbers are greater than 29, neither x nor y can be 1, so (x, y) = (11, 13). The numbers are 29 × 11 = 319 and 29 × 13 = 377, and their sum is 319 + 377 = 696.
- **A — 638:** Result of multiplying 29 × 22 after misidentifying the co-prime factors of 143.
- **B — 667:** Result of multiplying 29 × 23 due to an addition error in 11 + 13.
- **C — 725:** Result of multiplying 29 × 25 due to an addition error in 11 + 13.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Compute xy = 4147 ÷ 29 = 143 = 11 × 13; since both numbers > 29, Sum = 29 × (11 + 13) = 29 × 24 = 696.

### Q081 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The sum of two numbers is 360 and their HCF is 24. How many pairs of such numbers can be formed?

A. 2
B. 3
C. 7
D. 4

**Answer:** D. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4 (Correct):** Let the two numbers be 24x and 24y where x and y are co-prime. Given 24(x + y) = 360, we get x + y = 15. Listing unordered pairs (x, y) summing to 15: (1, 14), (2, 13), (3, 12), (4, 11), (5, 10), (6, 9), (7, 8). Excluding non-coprime pairs (3, 12), (5, 10), and (6, 9), exactly 4 co-prime pairs remain: (1, 14), (2, 13), (4, 11), and (7, 8).
- **A — 2:** Result of missing two of the four co-prime pairs summing to 15.
- **B — 3:** Result of overlooking one valid co-prime pair such as (4, 11) or (2, 13).
- **C — 7:** Result of counting all 7 unordered pairs summing to 15 without excluding non-coprime pairs.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide 360 ÷ 24 = 15 = 3 × 5; count numbers ≤ 7 co-prime to 15: φ(15)/2 = (2 × 4)/2 = 4 pairs.

### Q082 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

Three numbers are in the ratio 3 : 4 : 5 and their HCF is 17. What is their LCM?

A. 850
B. 960
C. 1020
D. 1190

**Answer:** C. 1020
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 1020 (Correct):** Let the three numbers be 3h, 4h, and 5h where h = 17 is their HCF. Since 3, 4, and 5 are pairwise co-prime, their LCM is h × LCM(3, 4, 5) = 17 × (3 × 4 × 5) = 17 × 60 = 1020.
- **A — 850:** Result of multiplying 17 × 50 instead of 17 × 60.
- **B — 960:** Result of multiplying 16 × 60 due to a calculation error.
- **D — 1190:** Result of multiplying 17 × 70 instead of 17 × 60.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Multiply the HCF by the LCM of the ratio terms: 17 × 60 = 1020.

### Q083 · Ratio & HCF / Co-prime Pairs

The sum of two numbers is 684 and their LCM is 3168. What is the difference between the two numbers?

A. 72
B. 108
C. 144
D. 180

**Answer:** B. 108
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 108 (Correct):** Let the numbers be Hx and Hy with gcd(x, y) = 1. Then Sum = H(x + y) = 684 and LCM = Hxy = 3168. Since gcd(x + y, xy) = 1, the HCF H equals gcd(684, 3168) = 36. Thus x + y = 684 / 36 = 19 and xy = 3168 / 36 = 88. Factoring 88 gives 11 × 8 = 88 with 11 + 8 = 19. Therefore, the difference between the two numbers is 36 × (11 − 8) = 36 × 3 = 108.
- **A — 72:** Result of multiplying 36 × 2 instead of 36 × 3.
- **C — 144:** Result of multiplying 36 × 4 instead of 36 × 3.
- **D — 180:** Result of multiplying 36 × 5 instead of 36 × 3.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use HCF = gcd(Sum, LCM) = gcd(684, 3168) = 36; then x + y = 19 and xy = 88 give (11, 8), so Difference = 36 × 3 = 108.

### Q084 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the least number which when divided by 12, 15, 20, and 27 leaves a remainder of 7 in each case.

A. 533
B. 540
C. 547
D. 557

**Answer:** C. 547
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 547 (Correct):** First find LCM(12, 15, 20, 27): 12 = 2² × 3, 15 = 3 × 5, 20 = 2² × 5, 27 = 3³. Thus LCM = 2² × 3³ × 5 = 4 × 27 × 5 = 540. The least number leaving a remainder of 7 in each case is LCM + 7 = 540 + 7 = 547.
- **A — 533:** Result of subtracting the remainder 7 from the LCM (540 − 7) instead of adding it.
- **B — 540:** Result of reporting the LCM (540) without adding the remainder 7.
- **D — 557:** Result of adding 17 instead of 7, or miscomputing the LCM as 550.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always compute LCM(divisors) + common remainder: 540 + 7 = 547.

### Q085 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the least number which when divided by 18, 24, 30, and 42 leaves remainders 11, 17, 23, and 35 respectively.

A. 2513
B. 2520
C. 2527
D. 2531

**Answer:** A. 2513
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2513 (Correct):** Notice that the difference between each divisor and its remainder is constant: 18 − 11 = 7, 24 − 17 = 7, 30 − 23 = 7, and 42 − 35 = 7. Next, LCM(18, 24, 30, 42) = 2³ × 3² × 5 × 7 = 8 × 9 × 35 = 2520. The required least number is LCM − common difference = 2520 − 7 = 2513.
- **B — 2520:** Result of reporting the LCM (2520) without subtracting the constant difference 7.
- **C — 2527:** Result of adding the constant difference 7 to the LCM (2520 + 7) instead of subtracting it.
- **D — 2531:** Result of adding the first remainder 11 to the LCM (2520 + 11).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When (divisor − remainder) = k is constant, the answer is simply LCM(divisors) − k = 2520 − 7 = 2513.

### Q086 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

What is the greatest 4-digit number which when divided by 12, 16, 24, and 36 leaves a remainder of 5 in each case?

A. 9931
B. 9936
C. 9949
D. 9941

**Answer:** D. 9941
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 9941 (Correct):** First compute LCM(12, 16, 24, 36) = 144. Divide the greatest 4-digit number 9999 by 144: 9999 = 144 × 69 + 63. Thus the greatest 4-digit multiple of 144 is 9999 − 63 = 9936. Adding the remainder 5 gives 9936 + 5 = 9941, which is still a 4-digit number.
- **A — 9931:** Result of subtracting the remainder 5 from 9936 instead of adding it.
- **B — 9936:** Result of reporting the greatest 4-digit multiple of 144 without adding the remainder 5.
- **C — 9949:** Result of miscalculating the remainder of 9999 ÷ 144 as 55 instead of 63.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Find the greatest 4-digit multiple of LCM(12, 16, 24, 36) = 144, which is 9936, then add 5 to get 9941.

### Q087 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the least number which when divided by 12, 15, 18, and 27 leaves a remainder of 4 in each case, and is completely divisible by 11.

A. 2704
B. 3784
C. 3244
D. 4324

**Answer:** B. 3784
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 3784 (Correct):** First find LCM(12, 15, 18, 27) = 540. Any number leaving remainder 4 when divided by these is of the form 540k + 4. Since 540 = 11 × 49 + 1, we have 540k + 4 ≡ k + 4 (mod 11). For divisibility by 11, k + 4 = 11, so k = 7. Thus the least such number is 540 × 7 + 4 = 3780 + 4 = 3784.
- **A — 2704:** Result of choosing k = 5 instead of k = 7 in 540k + 4.
- **C — 3244:** Result of choosing k = 6 instead of k = 7 in 540k + 4.
- **D — 4324:** Result of choosing k = 8 instead of k = 7 in 540k + 4.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use modular reduction: 540 mod 11 = 1, so k + 4 ≡ 0 (mod 11) gives k = 7 immediately, yielding 540 × 7 + 4 = 3784.

### Q088 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

What is the smallest 5-digit number which is completely divisible by 12, 15, 18, and 24?

A. 10080
B. 10020
C. 10280
D. 10440

**Answer:** A. 10080
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10080 (Correct):** First compute LCM(12, 15, 18, 24) = 360. Dividing the smallest 5-digit number 10000 by 360 gives 10000 = 360 × 27 + 280. To reach the next multiple of 360, add (360 − 280) = 80 to 10000, giving 10080.
- **B — 10020:** Result of adding 20 instead of 80, or miscalculating the LCM as 180.
- **C — 10280:** Result of adding the remainder 280 directly to 10000 instead of adding (360 − 280).
- **D — 10440:** Result of taking the 29th multiple of 360 (10080 + 360) instead of the 28th.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Smallest 5-digit multiple = 10000 + (LCM − remainder of 10000 ÷ LCM) = 10000 + (360 − 280) = 10080.

### Q089 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

What is the least number that must be added to 2000 so that the resulting sum is completely divisible by 12, 15, 18, and 25?

A. 200
B. 500
C. 700
D. 800

**Answer:** C. 700
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 700 (Correct):** First find LCM(12, 15, 18, 25) = 2² × 3² × 5² = 4 × 9 × 25 = 900. Dividing 2000 by 900 gives 2000 = 900 × 2 + 200. To make the sum divisible by 900, we need to reach 2700, so the least number to be added is 900 − 200 = 700.
- **A — 200:** Result of reporting the remainder 200 (the number to be subtracted) instead of the number to be added.
- **B — 500:** Result of miscalculating the LCM as 500 or 1250.
- **D — 800:** Result of subtracting 200 from 1000 instead of subtracting from the LCM 900.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Least number to add = LCM − (2000 mod LCM) = 900 − 200 = 700.

### Q090 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the least number which when divided by 8, 12, 16, and 20 leaves a remainder of 5 in each case, but when divided by 13 leaves no remainder.

A. 1205
B. 1685
C. 2165
D. 2405

**Answer:** D. 2405
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 2405 (Correct):** First find LCM(8, 12, 16, 20) = 240. The required number is 240k + 5. Since 240 = 13 × 18 + 6, we have 240k + 5 ≡ 6k + 5 (mod 13). Testing small k, 6(10) + 5 = 65 = 13 × 5, so k = 10 is the smallest positive integer solution. Thus the number is 240 × 10 + 5 = 2405.
- **A — 1205:** Result of choosing k = 5 instead of k = 10 in 240k + 5.
- **B — 1685:** Result of choosing k = 7 instead of k = 10 in 240k + 5.
- **C — 2165:** Result of choosing k = 9 instead of k = 10 in 240k + 5.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Reduce 240 mod 13 = 6; solve 6k + 5 ≡ 0 (mod 13) → k = 10, giving 240 × 10 + 5 = 2405.

### Q091 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the least number which when decreased by 7 is divisible by 15, 24, 28, and 35.

A. 847
B. 833
C. 840
D. 854

**Answer:** A. 847
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 847 (Correct):** A number that when decreased by 7 is divisible by 15, 24, 28, and 35 equals LCM(15, 24, 28, 35) + 7. Factorizing: 15 = 3 × 5, 24 = 2³ × 3, 28 = 2² × 7, 35 = 5 × 7, so LCM = 8 × 3 × 5 × 7 = 840. Therefore, the least number is 840 + 7 = 847.
- **B — 833:** Result of subtracting 7 from the LCM (840 − 7) instead of adding 7.
- **C — 840:** Result of reporting the LCM (840) without adding 7.
- **D — 854:** Result of adding 14 instead of 7 to the LCM.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** "Decreased by 7" means N − 7 = LCM, so add 7 to the LCM: 840 + 7 = 847.

### Q092 · Least Number & Remainders (LCM)

Find the greatest 3-digit number which when divided by 5, 6, 8, and 9 leaves remainders 2, 3, 5, and 6 respectively.

A. 714
B. 717
C. 720
D. 723

**Answer:** B. 717
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 717 (Correct):** The difference between each divisor and its remainder is constant: 5 − 2 = 3, 6 − 3 = 3, 8 − 5 = 3, 9 − 6 = 3. Compute LCM(5, 6, 8, 9) = 360. The greatest 3-digit multiple of 360 is 360 × 2 = 720 (since 360 × 3 = 1080). Subtracting the constant difference 3 gives 720 − 3 = 717.
- **A — 714:** Result of subtracting 6 instead of 3 from 720.
- **C — 720:** Result of reporting the greatest 3-digit multiple of 360 without subtracting the common difference 3.
- **D — 723:** Result of adding 3 to 720 instead of subtracting it.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Greatest 3-digit multiple of 360 is 720; subtract the constant difference 3 to get 717.

### Q093 · Greatest Divisor & Remainders (HCF)

Find the greatest number that divides 146, 317, and 269 leaving remainders 6, 9, and 17 respectively.

A. 14
B. 24
C. 28
D. 56

**Answer:** C. 28
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 28 (Correct):** Subtract each remainder from its respective dividend: 146 − 6 = 140, 317 − 9 = 308, and 269 − 17 = 252. Now find HCF(140, 308, 252): 140 = 28 × 5, 308 = 28 × 11, and 252 = 28 × 9. Since gcd(5, 11, 9) = 1, the greatest divisor is 28 (which is greater than all remainders).
- **A — 14:** Result of taking half of the true HCF (28 ÷ 2) by stopping common factor extraction early.
- **B — 24:** Result of an arithmetic error when subtracting remainders or computing the HCF.
- **D — 56:** Result of doubling the HCF (28 × 2), which does not divide 140 or 252.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Subtract remainders first, then take HCF: HCF(140, 308, 252) = 28.
