# Section IV: Advanced Mathematics & DI

## Chapter 9: Geometry

### Q516 · Lines and Parallel Angles

An angle is 24° more than half of its complementary angle. What is the measure of its supplementary angle?

A. 46°
B. 134°
C. 112°
D. 124°

**Answer:** B. 134°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 134° (Correct):** Let the angle be x. Its complement is (90° - x). Given x = (90° - x)/2 + 24°. Multiplying by 2: 2x = 90° - x + 48°, so 3x = 138° and x = 46°. Its supplement is 180° - 46° = 134°.
- **A — 46°:** Value of the angle itself instead of finding its supplementary angle.
- **C — 112°:** Obtained by solving x = (90 - x)/2 - 24 or miscalculating 180 - 68.
- **D — 124°:** Obtained by adding 24° directly to 90° and taking an incorrect supplement.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For angle x = (90 - x)/2 + k, directly use x = 30° + (2/3)k; here 30° + 16° = 46°, supplement = 134°.

### Q517 · Lines and Parallel Angles

Two parallel lines AB and CD have a point P between them such that ∠ABP = 38° and ∠CDP = 47° (with both rays PA and PC pointing toward the same side). What is the measure of the reflex angle ∠BPD?

A. 85°
B. 265°
C. 275°
D. 95°

**Answer:** C. 275°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 275° (Correct):** Draw a line PQ parallel to AB and CD through P. By alternate interior angles, ∠BPQ = ∠ABP = 38° and ∠DPQ = ∠CDP = 47°. Thus interior ∠BPD = 38° + 47° = 85°. The reflex angle ∠BPD = 360° - 85° = 275°.
- **A — 85°:** Obtained by finding the interior ∠BPD instead of the reflex angle.
- **B — 265°:** Result of subtracting 95° from 360° due to an arithmetic error in 38 + 47.
- **D — 95°:** Obtained by taking 180° - 85° (supplementary angle instead of reflex angle).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In a parallel-line M-vertex, interior angle = sum of the two acute angles; reflex = 360° - (α + β).

### Q518 · Lines and Parallel Angles

A transversal intersects two parallel lines such that a pair of co-interior (consecutive interior) angles are (2x + 17)° and (3x + 13)°. Find the measure of the larger of the two angles.

A. 103°
B. 77°
C. 117°
D. 133°

**Answer:** A. 103°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 103° (Correct):** Co-interior angles between parallel lines are supplementary, so (2x + 17) + (3x + 13) = 180. This gives 5x + 30 = 180, so 5x = 150 and x = 30. The two angles are 2(30) + 17 = 77° and 3(30) + 13 = 103°. The larger angle is 103°.
- **B — 77°:** Value of the smaller angle (2x + 17) instead of the larger angle.
- **C — 117°:** Obtained by equating (2x + 17) = (3x + 13) or taking 180° - 63°.
- **D — 133°:** Obtained if you set 5x + 30 = 360 instead of 180, giving a wrong x.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Co-interior angles sum to 180°, so sum the x-coefficients and constants directly: 5x = 150 → x = 30.

### Q519 · Lines and Parallel Angles

On a straight line AOB, rays OC and OD stand on the same side of AB such that ∠AOC : ∠COD : ∠DOB = 3 : 5 : 4. What is the angle between the angle bisectors of ∠AOC and ∠DOB?

A. 112.5°
B. 127.5°
C. 135°
D. 105°

**Answer:** B. 127.5°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 127.5° (Correct):** Since AOB is a straight line, ∠AOC + ∠COD + ∠DOB = 180°. Dividing 180° in the ratio 3 : 5 : 4 (total 12 parts), 1 part = 15°. Thus ∠AOC = 45°, ∠COD = 75°, and ∠DOB = 60°. The angle between the bisectors of ∠AOC and ∠DOB is (45°/2) + 75° + (60°/2) = 22.5° + 75° + 30° = 127.5°.
- **A — 112.5°:** Obtained by taking half of ∠COD along with the bisected outer angles.
- **C — 135°:** Obtained by taking 180° - 45° instead of summing the middle angle and half the outer angles.
- **D — 105°:** Obtained by adding only the two outer angles (45° + 60°) and ignoring ∠COD.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Angle between outer bisectors = 180° - (∠AOC + ∠DOB)/2 = 180° - 105°/2 = 127.5°.

### Q520 · Lines and Parallel Angles

Rays AB and CD are parallel and point in the same direction (to the right). Point E lies between the lines AB and CD (to the left of A and C) such that ∠BAE = 112° and ∠DCE = 126°. Find ∠AEC.

A. 58°
B. 118°
C. 122°
D. 238°

**Answer:** C. 122°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 122° (Correct):** Draw a line EF through E parallel to AB and CD, pointing to the right. Since AB || EF, co-interior angles ∠BAE + ∠AEF = 180°, so ∠AEF = 180° - 112° = 68°. Similarly, ∠CEF = 180° - 126° = 54°. Therefore, ∠AEC = 68° + 54° = 122°.
- **A — 58°:** Obtained by taking 180° - 122° instead of summing the two co-interior supplements.
- **B — 118°:** Result of an arithmetic slip when computing 180 - 112 or 180 - 126.
- **D — 238°:** Obtained by adding 112° + 126° directly without subtracting from 180°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When obtuse angles open toward the vertex between parallel lines, ∠AEC = 360° - (112° + 126°) = 122°.

### Q521 · Lines and Parallel Angles

Lines l₁ and l₂ are parallel. Line l₃ is perpendicular to l₁, and line l₄ intersects l₃ at an acute angle of 37°. What is the obtuse angle formed between l₄ and l₂?

A. 53°
B. 143°
C. 117°
D. 127°

**Answer:** D. 127°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 127° (Correct):** Since l₁ || l₂ and l₃ ⊥ l₁, we also have l₃ ⊥ l₂ (making a 90° angle with l₂). In the right triangle formed by l₂, l₃, and l₄, the acute angle between l₄ and l₂ is 90° - 37° = 53°. Hence the obtuse angle between l₄ and l₂ is 180° - 53° = 127°.
- **A — 53°:** This is the acute angle between l₄ and l₂ instead of the obtuse angle.
- **B — 143°:** Obtained by computing 180° - 37°, forgetting that l₄ makes 37° with l₃ (not l₂).
- **C — 117°:** Result of subtracting 37° from 90° inaccurately or taking 90° + 27°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Obtuse angle with l₂ = 90° + (angle with perpendicular l₃) = 90° + 37° = 127°.

### Q522 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

Each interior angle of a regular polygon is 135° more than its exterior angle. How many diagonals does this polygon have?

A. 16
B. 104
C. 120
D. 90

**Answer:** B. 104
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 104 (Correct):** Let interior angle be I and exterior angle be E. We know I + E = 180° and I - E = 135°. Subtracting gives 2E = 45°, so E = 22.5°. The number of sides is n = 360° / 22.5° = 16. Number of diagonals = n(n - 3)/2 = 16 × 13 / 2 = 104.
- **A — 16:** Number of sides of the polygon rather than the number of diagonals.
- **C — 120:** Obtained by using n(n - 1)/2 instead of n(n - 3)/2 (counting sides as diagonals).
- **D — 90:** Obtained by taking n = 15 (from an arithmetic error in 360 / 22.5).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** E = (180° - diff)/2 = 22.5°, n = 360°/22.5° = 16, diagonals = 16 × 13 / 2 = 104.

### Q523 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

The number of sides of two regular polygons are in the ratio 1 : 2, and their interior angles are in the ratio 3 : 4. Find the number of sides of the polygon with more sides.

A. 5
B. 8
C. 10
D. 12

**Answer:** C. 10
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 10 (Correct):** Let the number of sides be n and 2n. Their interior angles are 180°(n - 2)/n and 180°(2n - 2)/(2n) = 180°(n - 1)/n. The ratio is (n - 2)/(n - 1) = 3/4. Cross-multiplying: 4n - 8 = 3n - 3, so n = 5. The polygon with more sides has 2n = 10 sides.
- **A — 5:** Number of sides of the smaller polygon (n) instead of 2n.
- **B — 8:** Obtained by setting (n - 2)/(2n - 2) = 3/4 without dividing by 2n.
- **D — 12:** Obtained by assuming a dodecagon (150°) instead of solving the ratio.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For sides n and 2n, interior angle ratio is simply (n - 2)/(n - 1); here 3/4 directly gives n = 5, 2n = 10.

### Q524 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

If the sum of all interior angles of a convex polygon is 2340°, what is the number of diagonals in the polygon?

A. 75
B. 105
C. 65
D. 90

**Answer:** D. 90
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 90 (Correct):** Using (n - 2) × 180° = 2340°, we get n - 2 = 2340 / 180 = 13, so n = 15. The number of diagonals is n(n - 3)/2 = 15 × 12 / 2 = 90.
- **A — 75:** Obtained by using n(n - 5)/2 = 15 × 10 / 2 = 75 instead of n(n - 3)/2.
- **B — 105:** Obtained by using n(n - 1)/2 = 15 × 14 / 2 = 105 (including the 15 sides).
- **C — 65:** Obtained by forgetting to add 2 after dividing 2340 by 180 (using n = 13 in n(n - 3)/2 = 65).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Divide sum by 180° and add 2 to get n = 13 + 2 = 15; then 15 × 12 / 2 = 90.

### Q525 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

In a convex heptagon, four of the interior angles are 135° each, and the remaining three interior angles are in the ratio 2 : 3 : 4. What is the measure of the largest angle of the heptagon?

A. 120°
B. 135°
C. 160°
D. 180°

**Answer:** C. 160°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 160° (Correct):** The sum of interior angles of a heptagon (n = 7) is (7 - 2) × 180° = 900°. The sum of the four known angles is 4 × 135° = 540°. The remaining three angles sum to 900° - 540° = 360°. Dividing 360° in the ratio 2 : 3 : 4 (9 parts), 1 part = 40°, so the three angles are 80°, 120°, and 160°. Thus the largest angle is 160°.
- **A — 120°:** Value of the middle angle among the three unknown angles (3 parts = 120°).
- **B — 135°:** Value of the four given angles, assuming none of the unknown angles exceeds 135°.
- **D — 180°:** Obtained by using 1080° (octagon sum) instead of 900° (heptagon sum).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Heptagon sum = 5 × 180° = 900°; subtract 540° to get 360°, then 4/9 of 360° = 160°.

### Q526 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

A regular polygon has 54 diagonals. What is the measure of each interior angle of this polygon?

A. 135°
B. 144°
C. 150°
D. 160°

**Answer:** C. 150°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 150° (Correct):** Let the number of sides be n. Given n(n - 3)/2 = 54, so n(n - 3) = 108. Since 12 × 9 = 108, we get n = 12. Each exterior angle is 360° / 12 = 30°, so each interior angle is 180° - 30° = 150°.
- **A — 135°:** Interior angle of an octagon (n = 8), obtained if n(n - 3) is mis-factored.
- **B — 144°:** Interior angle of a decagon (n = 10), corresponding to 35 diagonals instead of 54.
- **D — 160°:** Interior angle of an 18-gon, obtained from using n(n - 1)/2 = 54 or wrong factor pair.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Solve n(n - 3) = 2 × 54 = 108 → n = 12; interior angle = 180° - 360°/12 = 150°.

### Q527 · Polygon Angles and Diagonals

If each interior angle of a regular polygon is 3.5 times its exterior angle, how many sides does the polygon have?

A. 7
B. 8
C. 9
D. 10

**Answer:** C. 9
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 9 (Correct):** Let each exterior angle be E. Then each interior angle is 3.5E. Since I + E = 180°, we have 4.5E = 180°, which gives E = 40°. The number of sides is n = 360° / 40° = 9.
- **A — 7:** Obtained by dividing 180° by 3.5 instead of 4.5 and rounding.
- **B — 8:** Obtained by taking E = 45° (using 4E = 180° instead of 4.5E = 180°).
- **D — 10:** Obtained by taking E = 36° from miscalculating 180 / 4.5.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** If I = kE, then n = 2(k + 1) directly! Here n = 2(3.5 + 1) = 9.

### Q528 · Triangle Inequality and Types

How many distinct triangles with integer side lengths can be formed such that the perimeter of each triangle is 13 cm?

A. 4
B. 6
C. 7
D. 5

**Answer:** D. 5
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 5 (Correct):** Let the sides be a ≤ b ≤ c with a + b + c = 13. By the triangle inequality, a + b > c, so 13 - c > c, meaning c ≤ 6. Also c ≥ 13/3, so c ∈ {5, 6}. For c = 6, (a, b) can be (1, 6), (2, 5), (3, 4). For c = 5, (a, b) can be (3, 5), (4, 4). Total = 5 triangles.
- **A — 4:** Obtained by missing one valid triplet such as (1, 6, 6) or (4, 4, 5).
- **B — 6:** Obtained by including a degenerate triplet such as (1, 5, 7) or (2, 4, 7) where a + b ≤ c.
- **C — 7:** Obtained by overcounting ordered triplets instead of distinct triangles.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For odd perimeter P, number of integer triangles = round((P + 3)² / 48) = round(256 / 48) = 5.

### Q529 · Triangle Inequality and Types

Two sides of a triangle are 8 cm and 15 cm. How many integer values are possible for the third side such that the triangle is obtuse-angled?

A. 10
B. 5
C. 12
D. 15

**Answer:** A. 10
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 (Correct):** Let the third side be x. By triangle inequality, 15 - 8 < x < 15 + 8, so 8 ≤ x ≤ 22. Case 1 (15 is longest, x ≤ 15): x² + 8² < 15² → x² < 161 → x ≤ 12, giving x ∈ {8, 9, 10, 11, 12} (5 values). Case 2 (x is longest, x > 15): x² > 8² + 15² = 289 → x > 17, giving x ∈ {18, 19, 20, 21, 22} (5 values). Total = 5 + 5 = 10 values.
- **B — 5:** Obtained by considering only one case (either x > 17 or x ≤ 12) and missing the other.
- **C — 12:** Obtained by including right-angled or acute boundary values like x = 17 or x = 13.
- **D — 15:** Total number of integer triangles possible with sides 8 and 15 (22 - 8 + 1 = 15) regardless of angle type.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always check BOTH cases for obtuse: x < √(b² - a²) and x > √(a² + b²), within (b - a, a + b).

### Q530 · Triangle Inequality and Types

Two sides of an acute-angled triangle are 9 cm and 12 cm. If the third side has an integer length, how many such triangles are possible?

A. 5
B. 6
C. 7
D. 17

**Answer:** C. 7
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 7 (Correct):** For an acute triangle, the square of the longest side must be less than the sum of squares of the other two sides. Case 1 (x ≤ 12): x² + 9² > 12² → x² > 63 → x ≥ 8, giving x ∈ {8, 9, 10, 11, 12} (5 values). Case 2 (x > 12): x² < 9² + 12² = 225 → x < 15, giving x ∈ {13, 14} (2 values). Total = 5 + 2 = 7.
- **A — 5:** Obtained by missing the two values where x > 12 (i.e., 13 and 14).
- **B — 6:** Obtained by excluding x = 12 (treating 12 as strictly smaller than the third side).
- **D — 17:** Total number of triangles of any type with sides 9 and 12 (20 - 4 + 1 = 17).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For acute triangle with sides a, b (a < b), integer x lies strictly between √(b² - a²) and √(a² + b²).

### Q531 · Triangle Inequality and Types

The lengths of two sides of an isosceles triangle are 7 cm and 16 cm. What is the perimeter of the triangle?

A. 30 cm
B. 30 cm or 39 cm
C. 39 cm
D. 23 cm

**Answer:** C. 39 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 39 cm (Correct):** In an isosceles triangle, the third side must be either 7 cm or 16 cm. If the sides were 7, 7, 16, then 7 + 7 = 14 < 16, violating the triangle inequality. Thus the sides must be 16 cm, 16 cm, and 7 cm, giving a perimeter of 16 + 16 + 7 = 39 cm.
- **A — 30 cm:** Obtained by choosing 7, 7, 16 without checking the triangle inequality (7 + 7 > 16 is false).
- **B — 30 cm or 39 cm:** Obtained by assuming both 7 and 16 can be the repeated side.
- **D — 23 cm:** Sum of only the two given sides (7 + 16) instead of all three sides.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Whenever a ≤ b/2 in an isosceles triangle, the smaller side cannot be repeated; perimeter = 2b + a.

### Q532 · Triangle Inequality and Types

The lengths of the three sides of a triangle are (x + 3) cm, (2x - 1) cm, and (3x - 4) cm. What is the minimum possible integer value of x?

A. 2
B. 3
C. 4
D. 5

**Answer:** B. 3
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 3 (Correct):** By the triangle inequality, the sum of any two sides must exceed the third. Checking (2x - 1) + (3x - 4) > (x + 3) gives 5x - 5 > x + 3, so 4x > 8 and x > 2. (The other two inequalities hold for all x > 0.) Since x is an integer and x > 2, the minimum integer value is x = 3 (giving sides 6, 5, 5).
- **A — 2:** Obtained by using ≥ instead of > in the triangle inequality, which gives degenerate sides 5, 3, 2.
- **C — 4:** Obtained by miscalculating 4x > 8 as x > 3.
- **D — 5:** Obtained by testing values without checking x = 3.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Plug in small integers directly: x = 2 gives 5, 3, 2 (fails 3 + 2 > 5); x = 3 gives 6, 5, 5 (valid).

### Q533 · Triangle Inequality and Types

The sides of a triangle are in the ratio 1/3 : 1/4 : 1/5. Which of the following correctly describes this triangle?

A. Acute-angled triangle
B. Right-angled triangle
C. Obtuse-angled triangle
D. Isosceles triangle

**Answer:** C. Obtuse-angled triangle
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — Obtuse-angled triangle (Correct):** Multiply the ratio 1/3 : 1/4 : 1/5 by LCM(3, 4, 5) = 60 to get integer side ratios 20 : 15 : 12. Comparing squares: 12² + 15² = 144 + 225 = 369, whereas 20² = 400. Since 12² + 15² < 20², the triangle is obtuse-angled.
- **A — Acute-angled triangle:** Obtained by assuming reciprocals of 3, 4, 5 form an acute triangle without squaring.
- **B — Right-angled triangle:** Obtained by confusing 1/3 : 1/4 : 1/5 with the 3 : 4 : 5 Pythagorean triplet.
- **D — Isosceles triangle:** Incorrect since all three side ratios are distinct.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Convert 1/3 : 1/4 : 1/5 → 20 : 15 : 12; since 12² + 15² = 369 < 400, it is obtuse-angled.

### Q534 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, AD is the internal bisector of ∠A meeting BC at D. If AB = 12 cm, AC = 16 cm, and BC = 14 cm, what is the length of BD?

A. 6 cm
B. 8 cm
C. 7 cm
D. 10.5 cm

**Answer:** A. 6 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 6 cm (Correct):** By the Internal Angle Bisector Theorem, BD / DC = AB / AC = 12 / 16 = 3 / 4. Since BD + DC = BC = 14 cm, we have BD = (3 / (3 + 4)) × 14 = (3 / 7) × 14 = 6 cm.
- **B — 8 cm:** Length of DC instead of BD.
- **C — 7 cm:** Obtained by assuming AD is a median that bisects BC into two equal halves.
- **D — 10.5 cm:** Obtained by computing (12 / 16) × 14 instead of (12 / 28) × 14.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** BD = BC × AB / (AB + AC) = 14 × 12 / 28 = 6 cm.

### Q535 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, AD is the internal angle bisector of ∠A meeting BC at D. If AB = 10 cm, BD = 4 cm, and DC = 6 cm, find the length of AC.

A. 12 cm
B. 16 cm
C. 24 cm
D. 15 cm

**Answer:** D. 15 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 15 cm (Correct):** By the Internal Angle Bisector Theorem, AB / AC = BD / DC. Substituting the given values: 10 / AC = 4 / 6 = 2 / 3. Therefore, AC = 10 × 3 / 2 = 15 cm.
- **A — 12 cm:** Obtained by adding the difference (6 - 4 = 2) to AB instead of taking the ratio.
- **B — 16 cm:** Obtained by adding AB + DC (10 + 6 = 16 cm).
- **C — 24 cm:** Obtained by using AC = (AB × BC) / BD = 100 / 4 - 1.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** BD : DC = 2 : 3, so AB : AC = 2 : 3; since 2 parts = 10 cm, 3 parts = 15 cm.

### Q536 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, the external angle bisector of ∠A meets side BC produced at E. If AB = 15 cm, AC = 10 cm, and BC = 6 cm, find the length of CE.

A. 4 cm
B. 9 cm
C. 12 cm
D. 18 cm

**Answer:** C. 12 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 12 cm (Correct):** By the External Angle Bisector Theorem, BE / CE = AB / AC = 15 / 10 = 3 / 2. Since E lies on BC produced, BE = BC + CE = 6 + CE. Thus (6 + CE) / CE = 3 / 2, giving 12 + 2·CE = 3·CE, so CE = 12 cm.
- **A — 4 cm:** Obtained by dividing 6 by 1.5 instead of solving (6 + CE)/CE = 3/2.
- **B — 9 cm:** Obtained by using the internal bisector ratio 3 : 2 on BC.
- **D — 18 cm:** Length of BE (6 + 12 = 18 cm) instead of CE.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For external bisector, CE = BC × AC / (AB - AC) = 6 × 10 / (15 - 10) = 12 cm.

### Q537 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, AB = 12 cm, AC = 18 cm, and BC = 15 cm. If AD is the internal angle bisector of ∠A meeting BC at D, what is the length of AD?

A. 9√2 cm
B. 6√3 cm
C. 9 cm
D. 12 cm

**Answer:** A. 9√2 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 9√2 cm (Correct):** By the Angle Bisector Theorem, BD / DC = 12 / 18 = 2 / 3. Since BD + DC = 15 cm, we get BD = 6 cm and DC = 9 cm. Using the angle bisector length formula, AD = √(AB × AC - BD × DC) = √(12 × 18 - 6 × 9) = √(216 - 54) = √162 = 9√2 cm.
- **B — 6√3 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 216 - 54 as 108 (√108 = 6√3).
- **C — 9 cm:** Obtained by taking only DC instead of computing AD.
- **D — 12 cm:** Obtained by taking the shorter side AB as the bisector length.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Length of internal bisector AD = √(AB·AC - BD·DC) = √(216 - 54) = 9√2 cm.

### Q538 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, AD is the internal bisector of ∠A meeting BC at D. If AB = 9 cm, AC = 15 cm, and the area of ΔABD is 24 cm², what is the area of ΔABC?

A. 40 cm²
B. 48 cm²
C. 64 cm²
D. 72 cm²

**Answer:** C. 64 cm²
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 64 cm² (Correct):** Since ΔABD and ΔADC share the same altitude from A, Area(ΔABD) / Area(ΔADC) = BD / DC. By the Angle Bisector Theorem, BD / DC = AB / AC = 9 / 15 = 3 / 5. Given Area(ΔABD) = 24 cm² (3 parts), 1 part = 8 cm². Thus Area(ΔABC) = (3 + 5) × 8 = 64 cm².
- **A — 40 cm²:** Area of ΔADC (5 × 8 = 40 cm²) instead of the total area of ΔABC.
- **B — 48 cm²:** Obtained by assuming AD is a median, which would double 24 to 48 cm².
- **D — 72 cm²:** Obtained by squaring the ratio (25/9 × 24 ≈ 66.7 or 72) as if ΔABD and ΔADC were similar.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Triangles with same height have areas in the ratio of bases: Area(ΔABD) : Area(ΔABC) = AB : (AB + AC) = 3 : 8.

### Q539 · Angle Bisector Theorem

In ΔABC, ∠BAC = 120°, AB = 6 cm, and AC = 12 cm. Find the length of the internal angle bisector AD of ∠BAC.

A. 3 cm
B. 4 cm
C. 4.5 cm
D. 6 cm

**Answer:** B. 4 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 4 cm (Correct):** Using the area method: Area(ΔABC) = Area(ΔABD) + Area(ΔACD). Thus (1/2)(6)(12)sin120° = (1/2)(6)(AD)sin60° + (1/2)(12)(AD)sin60°. Since sin120° = sin60°, cancelling (1/2)sin60° gives 72 = 18·AD, so AD = 4 cm.
- **A — 3 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 72 / 18 or dropping a factor.
- **C — 4.5 cm:** Obtained by taking the arithmetic mean (6 + 12)/4.
- **D — 6 cm:** Obtained by forgetting sin120° = sin60° or taking the shorter side AB.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For ∠A = 120°, AD is simply (b·c)/(b + c) = (6 × 12)/18 = 4 cm.

### Q540 · Triangle Centres

In ΔABC, I is the incentre of the triangle. If ∠BIC = 124°, what is the measure of ∠BAC?

A. 34°
B. 56°
C. 62°
D. 68°

**Answer:** D. 68°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 68° (Correct):** The angle subtended by side BC at the incentre I is ∠BIC = 90° + ∠A/2. Setting 90° + ∠A/2 = 124° gives ∠A/2 = 34°, so ∠BAC = 68°.
- **A — 34°:** Value of ∠A/2 instead of ∠A.
- **B — 56°:** Obtained by using the orthocentre formula 180° - ∠A = 124°.
- **C — 62°:** Obtained by using the circumcentre formula 2∠A = 124°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For incentre I, ∠A = 2(∠BIC - 90°) = 2(124° - 90°) = 68°.

### Q541 · Triangle Centres

In ΔABC, sides AB and AC are produced to D and E respectively. The bisectors of ∠DBC and ∠ECB intersect at O. If ∠BOC = 58°, find ∠BAC.

A. 32°
B. 116°
C. 122°
D. 64°

**Answer:** D. 64°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 64° (Correct):** The angle between two external angle bisectors at O is ∠BOC = 90° - ∠A/2. Thus 90° - ∠A/2 = 58°, giving ∠A/2 = 32° and ∠BAC = 64°.
- **A — 32°:** Value of ∠A/2 instead of ∠A.
- **B — 116°:** Obtained by using 2 × 58° instead of 2(90° - 58°).
- **C — 122°:** Obtained by using 180° - 58° (orthocentre formula).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For external bisectors meeting at O, ∠A = 2(90° - ∠BOC) = 2(32°) = 64°.

### Q542 · Triangle Centres

In an acute-angled triangle ΔABC, O is the circumcentre. If ∠OAB = 28° and ∠OCB = 34°, what is the measure of ∠ABC?

A. 56°
B. 62°
C. 68°
D. 124°

**Answer:** B. 62°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 62° (Correct):** Since O is the circumcentre, OA = OB = OC (circumradius). In isosceles ΔOAB, ∠OBA = ∠OAB = 28°. In isosceles ΔOCB, ∠OBC = ∠OCB = 34°. Since ΔABC is acute-angled, O lies inside the triangle, so ∠ABC = ∠OBA + ∠OBC = 28° + 34° = 62°.
- **A — 56°:** Obtained by doubling ∠OAB (2 × 28°) instead of adding ∠OBA and ∠OBC.
- **C — 68°:** Obtained by doubling ∠OCB (2 × 34°).
- **D — 124°:** Value of ∠AOC (2 × 62° = 124°) instead of ∠ABC.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In an acute triangle with circumcentre O, ∠B = ∠OAB + ∠OCB = 28° + 34° = 62°.

### Q543 · Triangle Centres

In an acute-angled ΔABC, H is the orthocentre and O is the circumcentre. If ∠BHC = 132°, what is the measure of ∠BOC?

A. 48°
B. 84°
C. 96°
D. 106°

**Answer:** C. 96°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 96° (Correct):** For orthocentre H, ∠BHC = 180° - ∠A, so 132° = 180° - ∠A, giving ∠A = 48°. For circumcentre O, ∠BOC = 2∠A = 2 × 48° = 96°.
- **A — 48°:** Value of ∠A itself instead of ∠BOC.
- **B — 84°:** Obtained by using ∠BHC = 90° + ∠A/2 (incentre formula) to get ∠A = 84°.
- **D — 106°:** Obtained by an arithmetic error when doubling 48°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In an acute triangle, ∠BOC = 2(180° - ∠BHC) = 2(180° - 132°) = 96°.

### Q544 · Triangle Centres

In ΔABC, medians AD and BE intersect at the centroid G. If the area of ΔBDG is 12 cm², what is the area of ΔABC?

A. 36 cm²
B. 48 cm²
C. 60 cm²
D. 72 cm²

**Answer:** D. 72 cm²
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 72 cm² (Correct):** The three medians of a triangle divide it into 6 smaller triangles of equal area. Since D is the midpoint of BC and G is the centroid, ΔBDG is one of those 6 equal-area triangles. Therefore, Area(ΔABC) = 6 × 12 = 72 cm².
- **A — 36 cm²:** Obtained by multiplying 12 by 3 (confusing 6 equal parts with 3 parts around G).
- **B — 48 cm²:** Obtained by multiplying 12 by 4 (confusing with the mid-point triangle).
- **C — 60 cm²:** Obtained by multiplying 12 by 5 due to miscounting regions.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Centroid G and midpoints divide ΔABC into 6 equal-area triangles, so Area(ΔABC) = 6 × Area(ΔBDG) = 72 cm².

### Q545 · Triangle Centres

The sides of a triangle are 10 cm, 24 cm, and 26 cm. What is the difference between its circumradius (R) and inradius (r)?

A. 4 cm
B. 13 cm
C. 17 cm
D. 9 cm

**Answer:** D. 9 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 9 cm (Correct):** Notice that 10² + 24² = 100 + 576 = 676 = 26², so it is a right-angled triangle with hypotenuse 26 cm. The circumradius R = H/2 = 26/2 = 13 cm. The inradius r = (P + B - H)/2 = (10 + 24 - 26)/2 = 4 cm. Thus R - r = 13 - 4 = 9 cm.
- **A — 4 cm:** Value of the inradius r alone instead of R - r.
- **B — 13 cm:** Value of the circumradius R alone instead of R - r.
- **C — 17 cm:** Sum R + r (13 + 4 = 17 cm) instead of the difference R - r.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For a right triangle, R - r = H/2 - (P + B - H)/2 = H - (P + B)/2 = 26 - 17 = 9 cm.

### Q546 · Triangle Centres

In an equilateral triangle of side 12√3 cm, what is the sum of its circumradius and inradius?

A. 6 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 18 cm
D. 24 cm

**Answer:** C. 18 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 18 cm (Correct):** The altitude of an equilateral triangle of side a is h = (√3/2)a = (√3/2) × 12√3 = 18 cm. In an equilateral triangle, the circumradius R = (2/3)h = 12 cm and the inradius r = (1/3)h = 6 cm. Therefore, R + r = h = 18 cm.
- **A — 6 cm:** Value of the inradius r alone.
- **B — 12 cm:** Value of the circumradius R alone.
- **D — 24 cm:** Obtained by doubling the circumradius (2R = 24 cm).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In any equilateral triangle, R + r equals the altitude h = (√3/2)a = 18 cm.

### Q547 · Triangle Centres

In ΔABC, medians BE and CF intersect at the centroid G at right angles. If BE = 9 cm and CF = 12 cm, find the length of side BC.

A. 10 cm
B. 15 cm
C. 7.5 cm
D. 12 cm

**Answer:** A. 10 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 cm (Correct):** The centroid G divides each median in the ratio 2 : 1 from the vertex. Thus BG = (2/3) × 9 = 6 cm and CG = (2/3) × 12 = 8 cm. Since BE ⊥ CF, ΔBGC is right-angled at G, so BC = √(BG² + CG²) = √(6² + 8²) = √100 = 10 cm.
- **B — 15 cm:** Obtained by taking √(9² + 12²) = 15 cm without multiplying the medians by 2/3.
- **C — 7.5 cm:** Obtained by taking half of 15 cm instead of 2/3.
- **D — 12 cm:** Obtained by taking the longer median CF as BC.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When medians BE ⊥ CF, BC = (2/3)√(BE² + CF²) = (2/3) × 15 = 10 cm.

### Q548 · Similar Triangles and BPT

If ΔABC ~ ΔPQR, the ratio of their perimeters is 3 : 5, and the area of ΔABC is 36 cm², what is the area of ΔPQR?

A. 60 cm²
B. 80 cm²
C. 100 cm²
D. 144 cm²

**Answer:** C. 100 cm²
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 100 cm² (Correct):** For similar triangles, the ratio of their areas is the square of the ratio of their corresponding sides (or perimeters). Thus Area(ΔABC) / Area(ΔPQR) = (3/5)² = 9/25. Given Area(ΔABC) = 36 cm², Area(ΔPQR) = 36 × (25/9) = 100 cm².
- **A — 60 cm²:** Obtained by using the perimeter ratio 3 : 5 directly (36 × 5/3) without squaring.
- **B — 80 cm²:** Obtained by miscalculating 36 × 25 / 9.
- **D — 144 cm²:** Obtained by squaring 12 (i.e. 12² = 144) instead of scaling by 25/9.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Area ratio = (Perimeter ratio)² = 9 : 25; 9 parts = 36 → 1 part = 4 → 25 parts = 100 cm².

### Q549 · Similar Triangles and BPT

In ΔABC, DE || BC with D on AB and E on AC. If AD : DB = 2 : 3, what is the ratio of the area of ΔADE to the area of trapezium DBCE?

A. 4 : 9
B. 4 : 25
C. 2 : 5
D. 4 : 21

**Answer:** D. 4 : 21
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 4 : 21 (Correct):** Since DE || BC, ΔADE ~ ΔABC. Given AD : DB = 2 : 3, we have AD : AB = 2 : (2 + 3) = 2 : 5. Thus Area(ΔADE) / Area(ΔABC) = (2/5)² = 4/25. Therefore, Area(trapezium DBCE) = 25 - 4 = 21 parts, so Area(ΔADE) : Area(DBCE) = 4 : 21.
- **A — 4 : 9:** Obtained by squaring AD : DB (2² : 3²) instead of using AB = AD + DB.
- **B — 4 : 25:** Ratio of Area(ΔADE) to Area(ΔABC) instead of to Area(trapezium DBCE).
- **C — 2 : 5:** Ratio of AD to AB without squaring for areas.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** If AD : DB = a : b, then Area(ΔADE) : Area(DBCE) = a² : ((a + b)² - a²) = 4 : 21.

### Q550 · Similar Triangles and BPT

In ΔABC, DE || BC where D lies on AB and E lies on AC. If AD = x, DB = x - 2, AE = x + 2, and EC = x - 1, find the value of x.

A. 3
B. 4
C. 5
D. 6

**Answer:** B. 4
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 4 (Correct):** By Basic Proportionality Theorem (Thales' Theorem), AD / DB = AE / EC. Thus x / (x - 2) = (x + 2) / (x - 1). Cross-multiplying: x(x - 1) = (x - 2)(x + 2), which gives x² - x = x² - 4, so x = 4.
- **A — 3:** Obtained by a sign error when simplifying x² - x = x² - 4.
- **C — 5:** Obtained by an algebraic error when expanding (x - 2)(x + 2).
- **D — 6:** Obtained by confusing x with AE = x + 2 = 6.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Cross-multiply x(x - 1) = x² - 4; x² cancels immediately leaving -x = -4 → x = 4.

### Q551 · Similar Triangles and BPT

In trapezium ABCD, AB || DC and AB = 2·DC. Its diagonals AC and BD intersect at O. If the area of ΔAOB is 84 cm², what is the area of ΔCOD?

A. 42 cm²
B. 28 cm²
C. 168 cm²
D. 21 cm²

**Answer:** D. 21 cm²
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 21 cm² (Correct):** Since AB || DC, ∠OAB = ∠OCD and ∠OBA = ∠ODC (alternate interior angles), so ΔAOB ~ ΔCOD by AA similarity. Thus Area(ΔAOB) / Area(ΔCOD) = (AB / DC)² = 2² = 4. Hence Area(ΔCOD) = 84 / 4 = 21 cm².
- **A — 42 cm²:** Obtained by dividing 84 by 2 instead of 2² = 4.
- **B — 28 cm²:** Obtained by dividing 84 by 3.
- **C — 168 cm²:** Obtained by multiplying 84 by 2 instead of dividing by 4.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Opposite triangles in a trapezium are similar: Area(ΔAOB) / Area(ΔCOD) = (AB/DC)² = 4.

### Q552 · Similar Triangles and BPT

In ΔABC, D is a point on side BC such that ∠ADC = ∠BAC. If AC = 12 cm and BC = 18 cm, what is the length of CD?

A. 6 cm
B. 8 cm
C. 9 cm
D. 10 cm

**Answer:** B. 8 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 cm (Correct):** In ΔABC and ΔDAC, ∠C is common and ∠BAC = ∠ADC, so ΔABC ~ ΔDAC by AA similarity. Therefore, BC / AC = AC / CD, which gives AC² = BC × CD. Substituting AC = 12 and BC = 18: 144 = 18 × CD, so CD = 8 cm.
- **A — 6 cm:** Obtained by taking half of AC or miscomputing 144 / 18.
- **C — 9 cm:** Obtained by taking half of BC.
- **D — 10 cm:** Value of BD (18 - 8 = 10 cm) instead of CD.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Remember the standard SSC result: when ∠ADC = ∠BAC, AC² = BC × CD → CD = 144 / 18 = 8 cm.

### Q553 · Similar Triangles and BPT

A vertical pole 6 m high casts a shadow 4 m long on the ground. At the same time, a nearby tower casts a shadow 28 m long. What is the height of the tower?

A. 18.67 m
B. 36 m
C. 42 m
D. 48 m

**Answer:** C. 42 m
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 42 m (Correct):** At the same time of day, the sun's angle of elevation is the same, forming similar right triangles. Thus Height_pole / Shadow_pole = Height_tower / Shadow_tower, so 6 / 4 = H / 28, giving H = 6 × 7 = 42 m.
- **A — 18.67 m:** Obtained by inverting the ratio (4/6 × 28 = 18.67 m).
- **B — 36 m:** Obtained by multiplying 6 × 6 instead of 6 × 7.
- **D — 48 m:** Obtained by multiplying 6 × 8.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Shadow scales by 28 / 4 = 7, so tower height = 6 × 7 = 42 m.

### Q554 · Similar Triangles and BPT

Two vertical poles of heights 12 m and 24 m stand on level ground. A line is drawn from the top of each pole to the foot of the other. At what height above the ground do the two lines intersect?

A. 6 m
B. 9 m
C. 18 m
D. 8 m

**Answer:** D. 8 m
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 8 m (Correct):** Using similar triangles, the height h of the intersection satisfies 1/h = 1/12 + 1/24 = 3/24 = 1/8, so h = 8 m. (Alternatively, h = ab/(a + b) = (12 × 24)/(12 + 24) = 288/36 = 8 m.)
- **A — 6 m:** Obtained by taking half of the smaller pole height.
- **B — 9 m:** Obtained by taking 3/4 of 12 instead of 2/3 of 12.
- **C — 18 m:** Arithmetic mean (12 + 24)/2 = 18 m instead of ab/(a + b).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Intersection height of crossed poles = ab/(a + b) = (12 × 24)/36 = 8 m.

### Q555 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In ΔABC, ∠BAC = 90° and AD ⊥ BC at D. If BD = 9 cm and DC = 16 cm, what is the length of AD?

A. 10 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 12.5 cm
D. 15 cm

**Answer:** B. 12 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 12 cm (Correct):** In a right-angled triangle, the altitude to the hypotenuse satisfies AD² = BD × DC. Thus AD = √(9 × 16) = √144 = 12 cm.
- **A — 10 cm:** Obtained by an arithmetic error in √144 or using 9 + 1.
- **C — 12.5 cm:** Arithmetic mean (9 + 16)/2 = 12.5 cm instead of geometric mean √(BD × DC).
- **D — 15 cm:** Value of AB = √(9 × 25) = 15 cm instead of AD.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Altitude to hypotenuse = √(BD × DC) = 3 × 4 = 12 cm.

### Q556 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In ΔABC, ∠ABC = 90°, AB = 24 cm, and BC = 32 cm. If BD is perpendicular to AC, find the length of BD.

A. 16 cm
B. 20 cm
C. 28 cm
D. 19.2 cm

**Answer:** D. 19.2 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 19.2 cm (Correct):** By Pythagoras' theorem, AC = √(24² + 32²) = √(576 + 1024) = √1600 = 40 cm (a 3-4-5 triplet scaled by 8). The altitude to the hypotenuse is BD = (AB × BC) / AC = (24 × 32) / 40 = 768 / 40 = 19.2 cm.
- **A — 16 cm:** Obtained by taking 2/3 of 24 instead of using ab/c.
- **B — 20 cm:** Value of half the hypotenuse (40 / 2 = 20 cm), which is the median to AC, not the altitude.
- **C — 28 cm:** Arithmetic mean (24 + 32) / 2 = 28 cm.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Altitude to hypotenuse = (a × b) / c = (24 × 32) / 40 = 19.2 cm.

### Q557 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In ΔABC, AB = 10 cm, AC = 14 cm, and BC = 16 cm. What is the length of the median AD drawn to side BC?

A. 2√21 cm
B. 4√5 cm
C. √82 cm
D. 12 cm

**Answer:** A. 2√21 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2√21 cm (Correct):** By Apollonius' Theorem, AB² + AC² = 2(AD² + BD²), where BD = BC/2 = 8 cm. Substituting: 10² + 14² = 2(AD² + 8²), so 296 = 2(AD² + 64). Dividing by 2 gives 148 = AD² + 64, hence AD² = 84 and AD = 2√21 cm.
- **B — 4√5 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 148 - 64 as 80 (√80 = 4√5).
- **C — √82 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 10² + 14² or subtracting 66 instead of 64.
- **D — 12 cm:** Arithmetic mean of AB and AC instead of using Apollonius' Theorem.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Median AD = √((AB² + AC²)/2 - (BC/2)²) = √(148 - 64) = √84 = 2√21 cm.

### Q558 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In ΔABC, the lengths of the sides are 8 cm, 10 cm, and 12 cm. What is the sum of the squares of its three medians?

A. 231 cm²
B. 308 cm²
C. 410.67 cm²
D. 154 cm²

**Answer:** A. 231 cm²
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 231 cm² (Correct):** For any triangle, 4(m₁² + m₂² + m₃²) = 3(a² + b² + c²). Here a² + b² + c² = 8² + 10² + 12² = 64 + 100 + 144 = 308. Therefore, m₁² + m₂² + m₃² = (3/4) × 308 = 3 × 77 = 231 cm².
- **B — 308 cm²:** Sum of squares of the sides (308 cm²) without multiplying by 3/4.
- **C — 410.67 cm²:** Obtained by multiplying 308 by 4/3 instead of 3/4.
- **D — 154 cm²:** Obtained by taking half of 308 instead of 3/4 of 308.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always use: Sum of squares of medians = (3/4) × (a² + b² + c²) = (3/4) × 308 = 231 cm².

### Q559 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In ΔABC, ∠B = 90°, and AD and CE are medians drawn to sides BC and AB respectively. If AD = 19 cm and CE = 22 cm, find the length of hypotenuse AC.

A. 24 cm
B. 26 cm
C. 28 cm
D. √845 cm

**Answer:** B. 26 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 26 cm (Correct):** In a right-angled triangle with ∠B = 90° and medians AD, CE to the legs, 4(AD² + CE²) = 5·AC². Substituting AD = 19 and CE = 22: 4(361 + 484) = 5·AC², so 4 × 845 = 5·AC², giving AC² = 4 × 169 = 676, and AC = 26 cm.
- **A — 24 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 4 × 169 or taking 2 × 12.
- **C — 28 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating the square root of 676.
- **D — √845 cm:** Obtained by using AD² + CE² = AC² instead of 4(AD² + CE²) = 5·AC².
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For medians AD, CE of right ΔABC: AC = √((4/5)(AD² + CE²)) = 2 × √(845/5) = 2 × 13 = 26 cm.

### Q560 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In an isosceles triangle ΔABC, AB = AC = 15 cm and BC = 18 cm. If D is a point on BC such that BD = 4 cm, what is the length of AD?

A. 11 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 13 cm
D. 14 cm

**Answer:** C. 13 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 13 cm (Correct):** For an isosceles triangle with AB = AC and D on base BC, Stewart's Theorem simplifies to AD² = AB² - BD·DC. Here DC = 18 - 4 = 14 cm, so AD² = 15² - 4 × 14 = 225 - 56 = 169, giving AD = 13 cm.
- **A — 11 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating 225 - 56.
- **B — 12 cm:** Height of the triangle from A to BC (√(15² - 9²) = 12 cm) rather than AD.
- **D — 14 cm:** Length of DC instead of AD.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** In isosceles ΔABC (AB = AC), cevian AD satisfies AD² = AB² - BD·DC = 225 - 56 = 169 → AD = 13 cm.

### Q561 · Pythagoras, Right Triangles and Medians

In a quadrilateral ABCD, the diagonals AC and BD intersect each other at right angles. If AB = 7 cm, BC = 9 cm, and CD = 6 cm, find the length of AD.

A. 2 cm
B. 4 cm
C. √166 cm
D. 8 cm

**Answer:** A. 2 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 2 cm (Correct):** When diagonals of a quadrilateral are perpendicular, the sums of squares of opposite sides are equal: AB² + CD² = BC² + AD². Substituting: 7² + 6² = 9² + AD², so 49 + 36 = 81 + AD², giving AD² = 85 - 81 = 4, and AD = 2 cm.
- **B — 4 cm:** Value of AD² instead of taking the square root AD.
- **C — √166 cm:** Obtained by adding 49 + 36 + 81 instead of subtracting 81.
- **D — 8 cm:** Obtained by using AB + CD = BC + AD (the tangential quadrilateral property) instead of squares.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For perpendicular diagonals: AD² = AB² + CD² - BC² = 49 + 36 - 81 = 4 → AD = 2 cm.

### Q562 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

ABCD is a cyclic quadrilateral in which ∠A = (3x + 12)° and ∠C = (2x - 7)°. What is the value of (∠A - ∠C)?

A. 35°
B. 54°
C. 63°
D. 117°

**Answer:** B. 54°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 54° (Correct):** In a cyclic quadrilateral, opposite angles are supplementary: ∠A + ∠C = 180°. Thus (3x + 12) + (2x - 7) = 180, so 5x + 5 = 180 and x = 35. Hence ∠A = 3(35) + 12 = 117° and ∠C = 2(35) - 7 = 63°. Therefore, ∠A - ∠C = 117° - 63° = 54°.
- **A — 35°:** Value of x instead of (∠A - ∠C).
- **C — 63°:** Value of ∠C alone instead of the difference.
- **D — 117°:** Value of ∠A alone instead of the difference.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Find x = 35 first, then ∠A - ∠C = (3x + 12) - (2x - 7) = x + 19 = 35 + 19 = 54°.

### Q563 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

Side AB of a cyclic quadrilateral ABCD is produced to point E. If ∠CBE = 82° and AD = DC, find the measure of ∠DAC.

A. 41°
B. 82°
C. 98°
D. 49°

**Answer:** D. 49°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 49° (Correct):** The exterior angle of a cyclic quadrilateral equals the interior opposite angle, so ∠ADC = ∠CBE = 82°. In ΔADC, since AD = DC, ∠DAC = ∠DCA = (180° - 82°) / 2 = 98° / 2 = 49°.
- **A — 41°:** Obtained by taking half of 82° instead of (180° - 82°)/2.
- **B — 82°:** Value of ∠ADC itself instead of ∠DAC.
- **C — 98°:** Value of ∠ABC (180° - 82°) instead of ∠DAC.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Exterior angle ∠CBE = ∠ADC = 82°; in isosceles ΔADC, ∠DAC = (180° - 82°)/2 = 49°.

### Q564 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

ABCD is a cyclic quadrilateral inscribed in a circle where AB is a diameter. If ∠ADC = 134°, what is the measure of ∠BAC?

A. 44°
B. 46°
C. 56°
D. 90°

**Answer:** A. 44°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 44° (Correct):** In cyclic quadrilateral ABCD, ∠ABC = 180° - ∠ADC = 180° - 134° = 46°. Since AB is a diameter, the angle in a semicircle is ∠ACB = 90°. In ΔABC, ∠BAC = 90° - 46° = 44°.
- **B — 46°:** Value of ∠ABC instead of ∠BAC.
- **C — 56°:** Obtained by miscalculating 180 - 134 as 34 and subtracting from 90.
- **D — 90°:** Value of ∠ACB instead of ∠BAC.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When AB is a diameter, ∠BAC = ∠ADC - 90° = 134° - 90° = 44°!

### Q565 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

ABCD is a cyclic trapezium such that AD || BC. If ∠ABC = 68°, what is the measure of ∠ADC?

A. 68°
B. 102°
C. 112°
D. 122°

**Answer:** C. 112°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **C — 112° (Correct):** In a cyclic quadrilateral, opposite angles are supplementary: ∠ABC + ∠ADC = 180°. Therefore, ∠ADC = 180° - 68° = 112°.
- **A — 68°:** Value of ∠BCD (which equals ∠ABC = 68° in a cyclic trapezium) instead of ∠ADC.
- **B — 102°:** Obtained by an arithmetic error in 180° - 68°.
- **D — 122°:** Obtained by subtracting 58° instead of 68° from 180°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Opposite angles in any cyclic quadrilateral sum to 180°: ∠ADC = 180° - 68° = 112°.

### Q566 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

In a cyclic quadrilateral ABCD, diagonals AC and BD intersect at P. If ∠DBC = 55° and ∠BAC = 45°, find the measure of ∠BCD.

A. 70°
B. 80°
C. 90°
D. 100°

**Answer:** B. 80°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 80° (Correct):** Angles in the same segment of a circle are equal, so ∠BDC = ∠BAC = 45°. In ΔBCD, the sum of angles is 180°, so ∠BCD = 180° - ∠DBC - ∠BDC = 180° - 55° - 45° = 80°.
- **A — 70°:** Obtained by miscalculating 180 - 100 or subtracting an extra 10°.
- **C — 90°:** Obtained by assuming ΔBCD is right-angled.
- **D — 100°:** Sum of the two given angles (55° + 45°) instead of subtracting from 180°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Use same-segment angles: ∠BDC = ∠BAC = 45°, so ∠BCD = 180° - (55° + 45°) = 80°.

### Q567 · Cyclic Quadrilaterals

In a cyclic quadrilateral ABCD, AB = 6 cm, BC = 8 cm, CD = 7 cm, DA = 9 cm, and diagonal AC = 10 cm. What is the length of diagonal BD?

A. 10.6 cm
B. 12.2 cm
C. 13.0 cm
D. 11.4 cm

**Answer:** D. 11.4 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 11.4 cm (Correct):** By Ptolemy's Theorem for a cyclic quadrilateral, AC × BD = AB × CD + BC × DA. Substituting the given sides: 10 × BD = (6 × 7) + (8 × 9) = 42 + 72 = 114. Thus BD = 114 / 10 = 11.4 cm.
- **A — 10.6 cm:** Obtained by misadding 42 + 72 or pairing sides wrongly.
- **B — 12.2 cm:** Obtained by an arithmetic slip when computing (42 + 72)/10.
- **C — 13.0 cm:** Obtained by taking (6 + 7) = 13 instead of using Ptolemy's product formula.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Ptolemy's Theorem: Product of diagonals = sum of products of opposite sides → BD = (42 + 72)/10 = 11.4 cm.

### Q568 · Chords and Circle Properties

Two chords AB and CD of a circle intersect internally at point P. If AP = 8 cm, PB = 6 cm, and CP = 4 cm, what is the total length of chord CD?

A. 10 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 14 cm
D. 16 cm

**Answer:** D. 16 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 16 cm (Correct):** By the Intersecting Chords Theorem, AP × PB = CP × PD. Thus 8 × 6 = 4 × PD, which gives PD = 12 cm. The total length of chord CD is CP + PD = 4 + 12 = 16 cm.
- **A — 10 cm:** Obtained by setting AP + PB = CP + PD (8 + 6 = 4 + PD).
- **B — 12 cm:** Length of segment PD alone instead of the full chord CD.
- **C — 14 cm:** Total length of chord AB (8 + 6 = 14 cm) instead of chord CD.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** AP × PB = CP × PD gives PD = 12 cm; always remember to add CP = 4 cm for full chord CD = 16 cm.

### Q569 · Chords and Circle Properties

Chords AB and CD of a circle are produced to intersect externally at point P. If AB = 7 cm, BP = 5 cm, and DP = 4 cm, what is the length of chord CD?

A. 8.75 cm
B. 11 cm
C. 15 cm
D. 19 cm

**Answer:** B. 11 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 11 cm (Correct):** Since P is external, PA = AB + BP = 7 + 5 = 12 cm. By the secant-secant theorem, PA × PB = PC × PD, so 12 × 5 = PC × 4, giving PC = 15 cm. Therefore, CD = PC - DP = 15 - 4 = 11 cm.
- **A — 8.75 cm:** Obtained by using AB × BP = CD × DP instead of PA × PB = PC × PD.
- **C — 15 cm:** Length of PC alone instead of CD.
- **D — 19 cm:** Obtained by adding PC + DP instead of subtracting DP from PC.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always measure from external point P: PA × PB = PC × PD → 12 × 5 = PC × 4 → CD = 15 - 4 = 11 cm.

### Q570 · Chords and Circle Properties

Two parallel chords of lengths 16 cm and 12 cm lie on opposite sides of the centre of a circle of radius 10 cm. What is the distance between the two chords?

A. 2 cm
B. 6 cm
C. 8 cm
D. 14 cm

**Answer:** D. 14 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 14 cm (Correct):** The perpendicular from the centre bisects each chord, giving half-chords of 8 cm and 6 cm. Their distances from the centre are d₁ = √(10² - 8²) = 6 cm and d₂ = √(10² - 6²) = 8 cm. Since they lie on opposite sides of the centre, the distance between them is 6 + 8 = 14 cm.
- **A — 2 cm:** Distance if the chords were on the same side of the centre (8 - 6 = 2 cm).
- **B — 6 cm:** Distance of only the 16 cm chord from the centre.
- **C — 8 cm:** Distance of only the 12 cm chord from the centre.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** When parallel chords of a 6-8-10 circle lie on opposite sides, distance = half-chord₁ + half-chord₂ = 8 + 6 = 14 cm.

### Q571 · Chords and Circle Properties

Two parallel chords of lengths 24 cm and 10 cm lie on the same side of the centre of a circle of radius 13 cm. Find the distance between the two chords.

A. 7 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 17 cm
D. 19 cm

**Answer:** A. 7 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 7 cm (Correct):** The half-chords are 12 cm and 5 cm. Using Pythagoras' theorem with radius 13 cm, their distances from the centre are d₁ = √(13² - 12²) = 5 cm and d₂ = √(13² - 5²) = 12 cm. Since they are on the same side of the centre, the distance between them is 12 - 5 = 7 cm.
- **B — 12 cm:** Distance of the 10 cm chord from the centre instead of the gap between chords.
- **C — 17 cm:** Distance if the chords were on opposite sides of the centre (12 + 5 = 17 cm).
- **D — 19 cm:** Obtained by subtracting half-chords incorrectly.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** On the same side of centre in a 5-12-13 circle, distance = |12 - 5| = 7 cm.

### Q572 · Chords and Circle Properties

Two chords AB and CD of a circle intersect at right angles at P. If AP = 4 cm, PB = 6 cm, CP = 3 cm, and PD = 8 cm, what is the radius of the circle?

A. (5√5)/2 cm
B. 5√5 cm
C. √65 cm
D. 6.5 cm

**Answer:** A. (5√5)/2 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — (5√5)/2 cm (Correct):** When two chords AB and CD intersect perpendicularly at P, the diameter D satisfies D² = AP² + PB² + CP² + PD². Thus 4R² = 4² + 6² + 3² + 8² = 16 + 36 + 9 + 64 = 125. Taking the square root, 2R = 5√5, so R = (5√5)/2 cm.
- **B — 5√5 cm:** Length of the diameter (2R) instead of the radius R.
- **C — √65 cm:** Obtained by miscalculating the sum of squares of the four segments.
- **D — 6.5 cm:** Obtained by averaging the two chord lengths or taking half of 13.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For perpendicular chords: Diameter = √(AP² + PB² + CP² + PD²) = √125 = 5√5 → Radius = (5√5)/2 cm.

### Q573 · Chords and Circle Properties

A chord AB of a circle is equal in length to the radius of the circle. What is the angle subtended by the chord at any point on the minor arc?

A. 30°
B. 60°
C. 120°
D. 150°

**Answer:** D. 150°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **D — 150° (Correct):** Since chord AB = OA = OB = radius, ΔOAB is equilateral, so the central angle ∠AOB = 60°. The angle subtended by AB on the major arc is 60° / 2 = 30°. Since opposite angles of a cyclic quadrilateral are supplementary, the angle on the minor arc is 180° - 30° = 150°.
- **A — 30°:** Angle subtended at the major arc instead of the minor arc.
- **B — 60°:** Central angle ∠AOB instead of the angle on the minor arc.
- **C — 120°:** Obtained by taking 180° - 60° instead of 180° - 30°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Chord = R → central angle 60° → major arc angle 30° → minor arc angle = 180° - 30° = 150°.

### Q574 · Tangents, Secants and Alternate Segment Theorem

From an external point P, a tangent PT and a secant PAB are drawn to a circle. If PT = 12 cm and PA = 8 cm, what is the length of chord AB?

A. 10 cm
B. 14 cm
C. 18 cm
D. 20 cm

**Answer:** A. 10 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 cm (Correct):** By the Tangent-Secant Theorem, PT² = PA × PB. Substituting PT = 12 and PA = 8: 12² = 8 × PB, so PB = 144 / 8 = 18 cm. Therefore, chord AB = PB - PA = 18 - 8 = 10 cm.
- **B — 14 cm:** Obtained by miscomputing 144 / 8 before subtracting PA.
- **C — 18 cm:** Length of the entire secant PB instead of chord AB.
- **D — 20 cm:** Obtained by adding PA + PT (8 + 12 = 20 cm).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** PT² = PA × PB gives PB = 144/8 = 18 cm; subtract PA = 8 cm to get chord AB = 10 cm.

### Q575 · Tangents, Secants and Alternate Segment Theorem

PA and PB are two tangents drawn from an external point P to a circle with centre O, touching the circle at A and B. If ∠APB = 54°, what is the measure of ∠OAB?

A. 27°
B. 36°
C. 54°
D. 63°

**Answer:** A. 27°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 27° (Correct):** In quadrilateral OAPB, ∠OAP = ∠OBP = 90°, so ∠AOB = 180° - ∠APB = 180° - 54° = 126°. Since OA = OB, ΔOAB is isosceles, giving ∠OAB = (180° - 126°) / 2 = 27°.
- **B — 36°:** Obtained by taking 90° - 54° directly.
- **C — 54°:** Value of ∠APB itself instead of ∠OAB.
- **D — 63°:** Value of ∠PAB = (180° - 54°)/2 = 63° instead of ∠OAB.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Always remember: ∠OAB = ∠APB / 2 = 54° / 2 = 27°!

### Q576 · Tangents, Secants and Alternate Segment Theorem

Line PAQ is tangent to a circle at A, and ΔABC is inscribed in the circle such that P and C lie on opposite sides of chord AB. If ∠BAP = 58° and ∠ABC = 72°, find ∠BAC.

A. 50°
B. 58°
C. 62°
D. 130°

**Answer:** A. 50°
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 50° (Correct):** By the Alternate Segment Theorem, the angle between tangent AP and chord AB equals the angle subtended by chord AB in the alternate segment, so ∠ACB = ∠BAP = 58°. In ΔABC, ∠BAC = 180° - 72° - 58° = 50°.
- **B — 58°:** Value of ∠ACB itself instead of ∠BAC.
- **C — 62°:** Obtained by an arithmetic error in 180° - (72° + 58°).
- **D — 130°:** Sum of ∠BAP and ∠ABC instead of subtracting from 180°.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Alternate Segment Theorem gives ∠ACB = ∠BAP = 58°; then ∠BAC = 180° - 130° = 50°.

### Q577 · Tangents, Secants and Alternate Segment Theorem

A circle is inscribed in ΔABC touching sides AB, BC, and AC at P, Q, and R respectively. If AB = 14 cm, BC = 16 cm, and AC = 12 cm, what is the length of AP?

A. 5 cm
B. 7 cm
C. 9 cm
D. 11 cm

**Answer:** A. 5 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 5 cm (Correct):** Tangent segments from a vertex to the incircle are equal, so AP = AR, BP = BQ, and CQ = CR. Summing all segments gives 2(AP + BC) = AB + BC + AC = 42, so AP + 16 = 21, and AP = 5 cm.
- **B — 7 cm:** Half of AB (assuming P is the midpoint of AB).
- **C — 9 cm:** Length of BP = s - AC = 21 - 12 = 9 cm instead of AP.
- **D — 11 cm:** Obtained by subtracting AP from BC (16 - 5 = 11 cm).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** For incircle tangent from vertex A, AP = s - BC = 21 - 16 = 5 cm.

### Q578 · Tangents, Secants and Alternate Segment Theorem

A circle is inscribed inside a quadrilateral ABCD, touching all four sides. If AB = 13 cm, BC = 18 cm, and CD = 15 cm, find the length of side AD.

A. 10 cm
B. 12 cm
C. 16 cm
D. 20 cm

**Answer:** A. 10 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 10 cm (Correct):** In a quadrilateral circumscribing a circle, the sums of opposite sides are equal: AB + CD = BC + AD. Substituting: 13 + 15 = 18 + AD, so 28 = 18 + AD, giving AD = 10 cm.
- **B — 12 cm:** Obtained by pairing adjacent sides incorrectly.
- **C — 16 cm:** Obtained by solving 13 + 18 = 15 + AD (pairing AB + BC = CD + AD).
- **D — 20 cm:** Obtained by solving 18 + 15 = 13 + AD (pairing BC + CD = AB + AD).
- **Formula / Shortcut:** Opposite sides sum equally: AD = AB + CD - BC = 13 + 15 - 18 = 10 cm.

### Q579 · Direct and Transverse Common Tangents

Two circles of radii 11 cm and 5 cm have their centres 10 cm apart. What is the length of their direct common tangent?

A. 6 cm
B. 8 cm
C. 10 cm
D. 12 cm

**Answer:** B. 8 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **B — 8 cm (Correct):** The length of the direct common tangent is √(d² - (r₁ - r₂)²), where d = 10 cm, r₁ = 11 cm, and r₂ = 5 cm. Thus DCT = √(10² - (11 - 5)²) = √(100 - 36) = √64 = 8 cm.
- **A — 6 cm:** Value of (r₁ - r₂) instead of the tangent length.
- **C — 10 cm:** Distance between the centres instead of the tangent length.
- **D — 12 cm:** Obtained by misapplying √(d² + (r₁ - r₂)²) or an arithmetic slip.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** DCT = √(d² - (r₁ - r₂)²): spot the 6-8-10 triplet immediately to get 8 cm.

### Q580 · Direct and Transverse Common Tangents

Two circles of radii 8 cm and 4 cm have their centres 15 cm apart. What is the length of their transverse common tangent?

A. 9 cm
B. 12 cm
C. √209 cm
D. 3√41 cm

**Answer:** A. 9 cm
**Notes & Option Breakdown:**
- **A — 9 cm (Correct):** The length of the transverse common tangent is √(d² - (r₁ + r₂)²), where d = 15 cm, r₁ = 8 cm, and r₂ = 4 cm. Thus TCT = √(15² - (8 + 4)²) = √(225 - 144) = √81 = 9 cm.
- **B — 12 cm:** Value of (r₁ + r₂) instead of the tangent length.
- **C — √209 cm:** Length of the direct common tangent √(15² - 4²) = √209 cm instead of transverse.
- **D — 3√41 cm:** Obtained by using √(d² + (r₁ + r₂)²) instead of subtracting.
- **Formula / Shortcut:** TCT = √(d² - (r₁ + r₂)²): 9-12-15 is a 3-4-5 triplet scaled by 3, so TCT = 9 cm.
